. In other words, the (partial)
\tau
-structures induced by
\phi
on
M
and
N
are equal (if a function of
M
takes an element out of
\phi(M),
we require that its
M
version also takes the element out, but not necessarily that the functions agree).

(2)

For
\iota\in\{1,2\}
a
\phi
-span
ι
is a triple
(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2})
such that
M_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{\ell}
,
\ell=1,2
,
\phi(M_{1})=\phi(M_{2})
and
\iota=1\Rightarrow\phi(M_{\ell})=\phi(M_{0})
and
\iota=2\Rightarrow\phi[M_{\ell}]\neq\phi[M_{0}]
.

(3)

A
\phi
-amalgam
ι
of a
\phi
-span
ι

(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2})
is a triple
(N,f_{1},f_{2})
such that
N\in\mathfrak{K}
and
f_{\ell}:M_{\ell}\xrightarrow[M_{0}]{}N
are such that
f_{1}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\phi(M_{1})=f_{2}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\phi(M_{2})
and
f_{1}\,{\upharpoonright}\,M_{0}=f_{2}\,{\upharpoonright}\,M_{0}.

(4)

We say that
M
is a
\phi
-amalgamation
ι
base (in
\mathfrak{K}
)
if
M\in\mathfrak{K}
and every
\phi
-span
ι

(M,M_{1},M_{2})
has a
\phi
-amalgam
ι
.

(5)

We say that
\mathfrak{K}
has the
\phi
-amalgamation
ι
property
if every
M\in\mathfrak{K}
is a
\phi
-amalgamation
ι
base.

(6)

We say that
N\in\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}
is
\phi
-universal
ι
if whenever
M\in\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}
and
f:\phi[M]\cong\phi[N]
, there exists
g:M\rightarrow N
which extends
f
.

(7)

We say that
\mathfrak{K}
has
\phi
-uniqueness
ι
if whenever
M_{0},M_{1},M_{2}\in\mathfrak{K}
are such that
M_{0}<_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{\ell}
,
\ell=1,2
, there is
f:\phi[M_{1}]\cong_{\phi(M_{0})}\phi[M_{2}]
and
f\,{\upharpoonright}\,M_{0}=\text{id}
.

(8)

We say that
\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}
is
\phi
-categorical
ι
if whenever
M,N\in\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}
, we have that
\phi[M]\cong\phi[N]
.

(9)

\mathfrak{K}
is
([\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\phi,\delta)
-continuous
when
if
\langle M_{\alpha}:\alpha<\delta\rangle
is
\leq_{\mathfrak{K}_{[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}}
-increasing continuous
then

M_{\delta}=\bigcup_{\alpha}M_{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{K}.
Omitting
\delta
means “for every limit ordinal
\delta
”.

Remark 5.7
.

(1)

Letting
\iota=1
and setting
\phi(x)
to be
x\neq x
, we get back the usual definitions of the amalgamation property and of a universal model.

(2)

Setting
\phi(x)
to be
x=x
in Definition
5.6
(8) we get back the usual definition of categoricity.

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{K}
and
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
be abstract classes such that
|\mathfrak{K}|=|\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}|
and
M\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N
implies
M\leq_{\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}}N
. Let
\phi
be a quantifier-free
\mathbb{L}_{\omega,\omega}(\tau(\mathfrak{K}))
-formula,
\iota\in\{1,2\}
and let
\Theta=[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]
be an interval of cardinals. Assume
2^{\lambda_{1}}=2^{<\lambda_{2}}<2^{\lambda_{2}}
.

1) If
\iota=1
and

(A)

\mathfrak{K}
is
[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
-continuous.

(B)

There is a
\phi
-universal
ι
model in
\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda_{2}}^{\ast}
.

(C)

\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda_{2}}
is
\phi
-categorical
ι
.

(D)

For any
\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
:

(a)

\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}
has
\phi
-uniqueness
ι
.

(b)

For any
\phi
-span
ι

\bar{M}=(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2})
in
\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}
, if
\bar{M}
has a
\phi
-amalgam
ι
in
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
, then
\bar{M}
has a
\phi
-amalgam
ι
in
\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}
.

Then
for any
M\in\mathfrak{K}_{[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}
, there exists
N\in\mathfrak{K}_{[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}
such that
M\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N
and
N
is a
\phi
-amalgamation
ι
base in
\mathfrak{K}_{\|N\|}
.

2) If
\iota=2,
we get the conclusion of part (1) if (A), (B), (C) there holds and

(D)
2

if
M_{\ast}\in\mathfrak{K}_{[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}
there is
\bar{N},N
such that:

(a)

N\in\{\phi(M)\colon M\in K_{\lambda_{2}}\},

(b)

\bar{N}=\langle N_{i}\colon i<\lambda_{2}\rangle
is a
\subsetneq
-increasing sequence of
\tau(\phi(M))
-models,

(c)

\bigcup_{i}N_{i}=N
and
i<\lambda_{2}\Rightarrow\|N_{i}\|<\lambda_{2},

(d)

N_{0}=\phi(M_{\ast}),

(e)

if
i<\lambda_{2},M_{0}\in\mathfrak{K}_{[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})},\phi(M_{0})=N_{i}
and
M_{0}\cap N=N_{i},
then there are
M_{1},M_{2}
such that
(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2})
is
\phi
-span
ι
and
\phi(M_{\ell})=N_{i+1}
for
\ell=1,2.

Proof.

1) Suppose
M
is a counter-example. We shall build an increasing continuous tree
\langle M_{\eta}:\eta\in{}^{\leq\lambda_{2}}2\rangle
such that for all
\eta\in{}^{\leq\lambda_{2}}2
:

\bullet_{1}

M_{\langle\rangle}=M
.

\bullet_{2}

M_{\eta}\in\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda+\rm{lg}(\eta)}
, where
\lambda=\|M\|.

\bullet_{3}

|M_{\eta}|\subseteq\lambda_{2}
.

\bullet_{4}

if
\iota=1,
then
\phi(M_{\eta})=\phi(M_{\nu})
for all
\nu\in{}^{\leq\ell(\eta)}2,
if
\iota=2
then
\phi(M_{\eta^{\smallfrown}\langle 0\rangle})=\phi(M_{\eta^{\smallfrown}\langle 1\rangle}).

\bullet_{5}

if
\iota=2,
then in
\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda_{1}+|\lg(\eta)|}
,
(M_{\eta},M_{\eta\smallfrown 0},M_{\eta\smallfrown 1})
is a
\phi
-span
ι
that has no
\phi
-amalgam
ι
.

\bullet_{6}

\langle M_{\eta\,{\upharpoonright}\,\varepsilon}:\varepsilon\leq\rm{lg}(\eta)\rangle
is
\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}
-increasing continuous.

We choose
M_{\eta}
for
\eta\in{}^{\alpha}2
by induction on
\alpha\leq\lambda_{2}:

Case 1
:
\alpha=0.
Let
M_{\langle\rangle}=M.

Case 2
:
\alpha=\beta+1
and
\eta\in{}^{\beta}2,M_{\eta}
cannot be a
\phi
-amalgamation
ι
base (by the choice of
M=M_{\langle\rangle}
) so by Definition
5.6
(4) there is a
\phi
-span
ι

(M_{\eta},M_{\eta^{\smallfrown}0},M_{\eta^{\smallfrown}1})
which has no
\phi
-amalgamation (for
\mathfrak{K}
) and
\|M_{\eta^{\smallfrown}\ell}\|=\|M_{\eta}\|=\lambda+|\rm{lg}(\eta)|.

It follows that
(M_{\eta},M_{\eta^{\smallfrown}0},M_{\eta^{\smallfrown}1})
has no
\phi
-amalgamation
ι
in
\mathfrak{K}^{*},
also
\phi(M_{\eta^{\smallfrown}\ell})=\phi(M_{\eta})=\phi(M_{\langle\rangle})
by Definition
5.6
(3). Renaming without loss of generality
|M_{\eta^{\smallfrown}1}|
is an ordinal so
<\lambda_{2}.

Case 3
:
\alpha
limit ordinal.

For
\eta\in{}^{\alpha}2
let
M_{\eta}=\bigcup\{M_{\eta\,{\upharpoonright}\,\beta}:\beta<\alpha\},
note
M_{\eta}\in K_{\lambda+|\rm{lg}(\eta)|}
by clause (1) of the assumption, and also
\phi(M_{\eta})=\bigcup\{M_{\eta\,{\upharpoonright}\,\beta}):\beta<\alpha\}=\phi(M_{\langle\rangle})
by the induction hypothesis. So we have carried the induction.

Let
A
be the
\tau(\mathfrak{K})
-structure induced by
\phi(M_{\eta})
for some (or any, see requirement
\bullet_{4})

\eta\in{}^{\lambda_{2}}2
. Let
\mathfrak{C}\in\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda_{2}}^{\ast}
be
\phi
-universal in
\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda_{2}}^{\ast}
. By
\phi
-categoricity (that is, clause (c) of the claim), pick
h:A\cong\phi(N)
. For each
\eta\in{}^{\lambda_{2}}2
, pick
g_{\eta}:M_{\eta}\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}
a
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
-embedding extending
h
. We have that
|M_{\eta}|\subseteq\lambda_{2}
, so extend each
g_{\eta}
arbitrarily to
f_{\eta}\in{}^{\lambda_{2}}\lambda_{2}
.

By Fact
5.5
, there exists
\eta\in{}^{\lambda_{2}}2
such that the set
S_{\eta}
described there is stationary. Let
C_{\eta}\coloneqq\{\delta<\lambda_{2}\mid|M_{\eta\,{\upharpoonright}\,\delta}|\subseteq\delta\}
. This is a club (by Lemma
5.1
applied to
\langle M_{\eta\,{\upharpoonright}\,\delta}:\delta<\lambda_{2}\rangle
,
\langle M_{\eta\,{\upharpoonright}\,\delta}\cap\delta:\delta<\lambda_{2}\rangle
), so let
\delta\in S_{\eta}\cap C_{\eta}
. We then must have that there is
\nu\in{}^{\lambda_{2}}2
such that
\eta_{0}\coloneqq\eta\,{\upharpoonright}\,\delta=\nu\,{\upharpoonright}\,\delta
,
\eta\,{\upharpoonright}\,(\delta+1)\neq\nu\,{\upharpoonright}\,(\delta+1)
, and (in particular),
g_{\eta}
and
g_{\nu}
agree on
M_{\eta_{0}}
. Moreover,
g_{\eta}
and
g_{\nu}
also extend
h
, so must in particular agree on
\phi(M_{\eta\,{\upharpoonright}\,(\delta+1)})
. This means that
(N,g_{\eta}\,{\upharpoonright}\,M_{\eta\,{\upharpoonright}\,(\delta+1)},g_{\nu}\,{\upharpoonright}\,M_{\nu\,{\upharpoonright}\,(\delta+1)})
is a
\phi
-amalgam of
(M_{\eta},M_{\eta\,{\upharpoonright}\,(\delta+1)},M_{\nu\,{\upharpoonright}\,(\delta+1)})
in
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
. By assumption (D)(b), this means there is such a
\phi
-amalgam in
\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda_{1}+|\delta|}
, a contradiction to requirement
\bullet_{5}
.

2) Similarly.
∎

6.
Good frames

Good frames are a local notion of “bare bone” superstability, introduced by the first author
[
She09a
, Chapter II]
. Essentially, an AEC
\mathfrak{K}
has a
good
\lambda
-frame
if it looks superstable in
\lambda
in the sense that
\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}
has some reasonable structural properties (like amalgamation), and there is a forking-like notion for types of singletons over models.

In this section, we give the definition we will use and state some fundamental results (most of them known or folklore) about good frames.

The definition we give here allows good frames in several cardinals (as in
[
Vas16b
, 2.21]
) but using fragmented AECs (so also allowing “shrinking frames”,
[
Vas17b
, Appendix A]
). The frames in this paper will always be type-full (i.e. all nonalgebraic types will be basic), so we will drop the adjective and ignore basic types in the definition.

Definition 6.1
.

A
(type-full) good frame
(or a semi good frame) is a pair
\mathfrak{s}=(\mathfrak{K},F)=(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{s}},F_{\mathfrak{s}})
, where:

(1)

\mathfrak{K}
is a fragmented AEC (or a semi fragmented AEC) such that:

(a)

\mathfrak{K}\neq\emptyset
.

(b)

\mathfrak{K}_{<\text{LS}(\mathfrak{K})}=\emptyset
.

(c)

\mathfrak{K}
has amalgamation, joint embedding, and no maximal models.

(d)

For every
M\in\mathfrak{K}
, there is
N\in\mathfrak{K}
such that
N
is universal over
M
and
\|N\|=\|M\|
.

(2)

F
is a binary relation taking as input pairs
(p,M)
, where
p
is an orbital type and
M
is a model in
\mathfrak{K}
. We write
p
does not fork over
M
(or
p
does not
\mathfrak{s}
-fork over
M
) instead of
F(p,M)
and require that
F
satisfies the following:

(a)

If
p
does not fork over
M
, then
p\in\mathscr{S}(N)
for some
N\geq_{\mathfrak{K}}M
.

(b)

Invariance: if
f:N\cong N^{\prime}
and
p\in\mathscr{S}(N)
does not fork over
M
, then
f(p)
does not fork over
f[M]
.

(c)

Monotonicity: if
p\in\mathscr{S}(N)
does not fork over
M
and
M\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M^{\prime}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N
, then
p\,{\upharpoonright}\,M^{\prime}
does not fork over
M
and
p
does not fork over
M^{\prime}
.

(d)

Disjointness: if
p\in\mathscr{S}(N)
does not fork over
M
, then
p\,{\upharpoonright}\,M
is algebraic if and only if
p
is algebraic.

(e)

Extension: for any
M\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N
and any
p\in\mathscr{S}(M)
there exists
q\in\mathscr{S}(N)
such that
q
extends
p
and
q
does not fork over
M
.

(f)

Uniqueness: for any
M\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N
and any
p,q\in\mathscr{S}(N)
, if both
p
and
q
do not fork over
M
and
p\,{\upharpoonright}\,M=q\,{\upharpoonright}\,M
, then
p=q
.

(g)

Local character: If
\langle M_{i}:i\leq\delta\rangle
is increasing continuous in
\mathfrak{K}
and
p\in\mathscr{S}(M_{\delta})
, then there exists
i<\delta
such that
p
does not fork over
M_{i}
.

(h)

Symmetry: If
p=\mathbf{tp}(a/N;N^{\prime})
does not fork over
M
and
b\in|N|
, then there exists
M^{\prime},N^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{K}
such that
N^{\prime}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N^{\prime\prime}
,
M\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M^{\prime}
,
a\in M^{\prime}
, and
\mathbf{tp}(b/M^{\prime};N^{\prime\prime})
does not fork over
M
.

We write
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{s}}
for the class of the frame
\mathfrak{s}
. We say that
\mathfrak{s}
is
on
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
if
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{s}}=\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
. The
domain
of a good frame
\mathfrak{s}
is the domain of
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{s}}
(see Definition
3.6
). For
\lambda\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{s})
, we say that
\mathfrak{s}
is
categorical in
\lambda
if
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{s}}
is categorical in
\lambda
. We say that
\mathfrak{s}
is
categorical
if it is categorical in
all

\lambda\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{s})
. We also define restrictions to smaller classes of models such as
\mathfrak{s}_{\lambda}
in the natural way. We say that
\mathfrak{s}
is a
good
\lambda
-frame
if
\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{s}_{\lambda}
.

We will use the following construction of a good frame:

Fact 6.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{K}
be an AEC with arbitrarily large models. Let
\mu>\text{LS}(\mathfrak{K})
and let
\theta\geq\mu
. Assume that
\mathfrak{K}_{<\theta}
has amalgamation and no maximal models. Assume further that
\mathfrak{K}
is categorical in
\mu
and
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{sat}}_{(\text{LS}(\mathfrak{K}),\theta)}
is
\text{LS}(\mathfrak{K})
-tame. Then there is a (categorical) good frame on
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{sat}}_{(\text{LS}(\mathfrak{K}),\theta)}
.

Proof.

By
[
Vas17c
, 5.7(1)]
,
\mathfrak{K}
is
\text{LS}(\mathfrak{K})
-superstable and has
\text{LS}(\mathfrak{K})
-symmetry. The result now follows from the proof of
[
Vas17b
, A.3]
.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

Combining local character, transitivity, and uniqueness, we have that whenever
\mathfrak{s}
is a good frame, then
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{s}}
is
\min(\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{s}))
-tame.

Regarding disjointness, it follows from the other properties if the frame is categorical:

Lemma 6.4
.

If
\mathfrak{s}
satisfies all the properties of good frames, except perhaps disjointness, and
\mathfrak{s}
is categorical, then
\mathfrak{s}
satisfies disjointness as well.

Proof.

By the conjugation property
[
She09a
, III.1.21]
(whose proof never uses disjointness).
∎

We now state and prove canonicity of the framework. First, frames with the same restriction in their low cardinals are the same:

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{s}
and
\mathfrak{t}
be good frames with
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{s}}=\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{t}}
. Let
\lambda\coloneqq\min(\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{s}))
. If
\mathfrak{s}_{\lambda}=\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda}
, then
\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{t}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{K}\coloneqq\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{s}}=\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{t}}
. Let
M\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N
be in
\mathfrak{K}
and let
p\in\mathscr{S}(N)
. Assume that
p
does not
\mathfrak{s}
-fork over
M
. We show that
p
does not
\mathfrak{t}
-fork over
M
, and the converse is symmetric. First, by local character and transitivity there exists
M_{0}\in\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}
such that
p
does not
\mathfrak{s}
-fork over
M_{0}
. In particular (by monotonicity), for every
N_{0}\in\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}
with
M_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N
,
p\,{\upharpoonright}\,N_{0}
does not
\mathfrak{s}
-fork over
M_{0}
. Since
\mathfrak{s}_{\lambda}=\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda}
,
p\,{\upharpoonright}\,N_{0}
does not
\mathfrak{t}
-fork over
M_{0}
. Now pick
N_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}
such that
p
does not
\mathfrak{t}
-fork over
N_{0}^{\prime}
and (by monotonicity), enlarge it so that
M_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N_{0}^{\prime}
. Then by transitivity,
p
does not
\mathfrak{t}
-fork over
M_{0}
, hence (by monotonicity) over
M
, as desired.
∎

Remark 6.6
.

Disjointness and symmetry are not used in the proof. Regarding local character, we only use that any type does not fork over a model of size
\lambda
.

Fact 6.7

(Canonicity of categorical good frames)
.

Let
\mathfrak{s}
and
\mathfrak{t}
be categorical good frames with
\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{s})=\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{t})
. Let
\lambda\coloneqq\min(\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{s}))
. If
\left(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{s}}\right)_{\lambda}=\left(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{t}}\right)_{\lambda}
, then
\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{t}
.

Proof.

By
[
Vas16a
, 9.7]
,
\mathfrak{s}_{\lambda}=\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda}
. By canonicity of categorical fragmented AECs (Theorem
3.22
),
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{s}}=\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{t}}
. By Lemma
6.5
,
\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{t}
.
∎

Categoricity of a good frame may seem a strong assumption. However, we have:

Fact 6.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{s}
be a good frame and let
\lambda\coloneqq\min(\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{s}))
. If
\mathfrak{s}
is categorical in
\lambda
, then there is a categorical good frame
\mathfrak{t}
with
\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{t})=\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{s})
and
\left(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{t}}\right)_{\lambda}=\left(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{s}}\right)_{\lambda}
.

Proof.

By the proof of
[
Vas17b
, A.2]
: that is, just restrict to saturated models (note that this gives fragmented AEC, not necessarily an AEC, but this is allowed by the above definition of a good frame)
∎

We recall the following result about categoricity transfers in good frames:

Discussion 6.9
.

(1) The following assertion appears as a claim in earlier versions, but the referee point out that saying
[
Vas17b
, Theorem A.9]
is not convincing as there
\mathfrak{K}
is not fragmented. As we prefer not to resolve it, we change the proof of
14.3
, the only place it was used.

(2) The assertion was:

Assume that
\mathfrak{s}
is a good frame with fragmented AEC
\mathfrak{K}
. Let
\Theta\coloneqq\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{K})
, let
\lambda\coloneqq\min(\Theta)
, and let
\mu\in\Theta
. Assume that
\mathfrak{K}
is categorical in
\lambda
.

Let
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
be the AEC generated by
\mathfrak{K}_{\mu}
. If
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
is categorical in
\mu^{+}
, it follows that:

(A)

\mathfrak{K}
is
\theta
-continuous for every
\theta\in\Theta
.

(B)

\mathfrak{K}
is categorical in every
\mu^{\prime}\in\Theta
.

(3) The asserted proof was: we argue by Fact
6.8
, we might as well assume that
\mathfrak{s}
is categorical. We are then in the setup of
[
Vas17b
, Theorem A.9]
, which gives the result we want.

We will also use the following upward frame transfer:

Definition 6.10

(
[
She09a
, II.2.4,II.2.5]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{s}
be a good
\lambda
-frame and let
\mathfrak{K}
be the AEC generated by
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{s}}
. We let
\mathfrak{s}^{\rm{up}}\coloneqq(\mathfrak{K},F)
, where
F
is the following binary relation on pairs
(p,M)
, with
p
is an orbital type and
M
is a model in
\mathfrak{K}
:
F(p,M)
if and only if
p\in\mathscr{S}(N)
for some
M\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N
, and there exists
M_{0}\in\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{s}}
such that
M_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M
and for all
N_{0}\in\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{s}}
with
M_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N
,
p\,{\upharpoonright}\,N_{0}
does not
\mathfrak{s}
-fork over
M_{0}
.

Fact 6.11
.

Let
\mathfrak{s}
be a good
\lambda
-frame.

(1)

[
She09a
, II.2.11]

\mathfrak{s}^{\rm{up}}
satisfies all the axioms from the definition of a type-full good frame, except for (
1c
),(
1d
), (
2e
), (
2f
), and (
2h
) in Definition
6.1
.

(2)

[
BV17
, 6.9]
If
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\rm{up}}}
is
\lambda
-tame and has amalgamation, then
\mathfrak{s}^{\rm{up}}
is a good frame.

7.
Two-dimensional independence notions

While good frames describe a forking-like relation for types of
singletons
over models, two-dimensional independence notions describe a forking-like relations for types of models over models. At that point, it seems more convenient to think of such a relation as a
4
-ary relation on squares of models
9
9

9

The referee asks: why does 4-ary correspond to
2
-dimensional? For the same reason that a square is two-dimensional despite having four vertices:
4=2^{2}.
More explicitly,
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
is a
4
-place relation, and
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},M_{3})
it implies
M_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{\ell}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{3}
(and
M_{1}\cap M_{2}=M_{0}
). But we can express this as “
M_{1},M_{2}
are independent over
M_{0}
inside
M_{3}
”saying it in this form, it appear as a
2
-place relation. Alternatively we can write “
\bar{N}=\langle N_{u}:u\in\mathcal{P}(n)\rangle
is independent” where
N_{\emptyset}=M_{0},N_{\{0\}}=M_{1},N_{\{1\}}=M_{2},N_{\{0,1\}}=M_{3};
in this form the
2
appear in
\mathcal{P}(2)
and later generalized to
\langle N_{u}:u\in\mathcal{P}(n)\rangle
and is called
n
-dimensional and is used to analyze models in
\lambda^{+n}
.
. Squares in nonforking amalgamation form the simplest nontrivial example of an independent system (a concept defined in the next section).

This section studies abstract frameworks for two-dimensional independence, while the next section will look at higher dimensions. We state the important properties of two-dimensional independence relations. We look also at their canonicity (Theorem
7.14
). Two key questions are when a two-dimensional independence relation can be built from a good frame, and when a two-dimensional independence relation for models of size
\lambda
implies the existence of a good
\lambda^{+}
-frame (these themes are present already in
[
She09a
, II, III]
). Frames that are well-behaved in these respects are called
extendible
(Definition
7.15
). A frame that can be extended
\omega
-many steps is called
(<\omega)
-extendible
. These correspond (but are slightly more convenient to work with than) the
\omega
-successful good frames in
[
She09a
, III]
. We give conditions under which such frames exist, both in the compact (Fact
7.21
) and non-compact (Fact
7.16
) cases. For the latter result, the weak diamond (a weakening of the generalized continuum hypothesis) is assumed.

Definition 7.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{K}
be an abstract class. A
two-dimensional independence relation (or notion) on
\mathfrak{K}
is a
4
-ary relation
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
on
\mathfrak{K}
satisfying:

(A)

\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},M_{3})
implies
M_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{\ell}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{3}
for
\ell=1,2
. We may write
M_{1}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{M_{3}}M_{2}
instead of
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},M_{3})
.

(B)

If
M_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{\ell}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{3}
,
\ell=1,2
and
f:M_{3}\rightarrow M_{3}^{\prime}
is a
\mathfrak{K}
-embedding, then
M_{1}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{M_{3}}M_{2}
if and only if
f[M_{1}]\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{f[M_{0}]}^{M_{3}^{\prime}}f[M_{2}]
.

(C)

Monotonicity: if
M_{1}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{M_{3}}M_{2}
and
M_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{1}^{\prime}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{1}
, then
M_{1}^{\prime}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{M_{3}}M_{2}
.

(D)

Disjointness: if
M_{1}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{M_{3}}M_{2}
, then
M_{1}\cap M_{2}=M_{0}
.

(E)

Symmetry: if
M_{1}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{M_{3}}M_{2}
, then
M_{2}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{M_{3}}M_{1}
.

(F)

Transitivity: if
M_{1}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{M_{3}}M_{2}
and
M_{3}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{2}}^{M_{5}}M_{4}
, then
M_{1}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{M_{5}}M_{4}
.

(G)

Extension: whenever
M_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{\ell}
,
\ell=1,2
, there exists
M_{3}\in\mathfrak{K}
and
f_{\ell}:M_{\ell}\xrightarrow[M_{0}]{}M_{3}
such that
f_{1}[M_{1}]\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{M_{3}}f_{2}[M_{2}]
.

(H)

Uniqueness: whenever
M_{1}^{\ell}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}^{\ell}}^{M_{3}^{\ell}}M_{2}^{\ell}
for
\ell=0,1
and
f_{k}:M_{k}^{0}\cong M_{k}^{1}
,
k<3
are such that
f_{0}\subseteq f_{1}
,
f_{0}\subseteq f_{2}
, then there exists
M_{3}^{2}\in\mathfrak{K}
with
M_{3}^{1}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{3}^{2}
and and
f_{3}:M_{3}^{0}\rightarrow M_{3}^{2}
such that
f_{k}\subseteq f_{3}
for all
k<3
.

Definition 7.2
.

Let
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
be a two-dimensional independence notion on
\mathfrak{K}
. If
\mathfrak{s}
is a good frame on
\mathfrak{K}
, we say that
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits

respects
\mathfrak{s}
if whenever
M_{1}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{M_{3}}M_{2}
and
a\in|M_{1}|
, we have that
\mathbf{tp}(a/M_{2};M_{3})
does not
\mathfrak{s}
-fork over
M_{0}
.

As in Lemma
6.4
, note that disjointness is not really needed in some cases:

Lemma 7.3
.

Assume that
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
satisfies all the properties of a two-dimensional independence notion on
\mathfrak{K}
, except perhaps for disjointness. Assume that
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
respects a good frame
\mathfrak{s}
on
\mathfrak{K}
. Then
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
satisfies disjointness.

Proof.

Because
\mathfrak{s}
satisfies disjointness.
∎

It is sometimes useful to extend
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
to take sets on the left and right hand sides. This is the content of the next definition, versions of which were already considered by both the first author
[
She09a
, II.6.35]
and the second author (in joint work with Boney, Grossberg, and Kolesnikov)
[
BGKV16
]
. See also the recent work of Lieberman, Rosický, and the second author
[
LRV
, 8.2]
.

Definition 7.4
.

Let
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
be a two-dimensional independence relation on
\mathfrak{K}
.

(1)

Define a
4
-ary relation
\overline{\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits}
as follows:
A\overset{N}{\underset{M}{\overline{\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits}}}B
holds if and only if
M\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N
,
A,B\subseteq|N|
, and there exists
M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}\in\mathfrak{K}
such that
N\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{3}
,
A\subseteq|M_{1}|
,
B\subseteq|M_{2}|
, and
M_{1}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}^{M_{3}}M_{2}
.

(2)

For
p
an orbital type, we say that
p

does not fork over
M
if
M\in\mathfrak{K}
, and there exists
b,A,M_{3}
such that
p=\mathbf{tp}(b/A;M_{3})
and
b\overset{M_{3}}{\underset{M}{\overline{\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits}}}A
.

(3)

Define a binary relation
F=F(\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits)
taking as input pairs
(p,M)
, where
p
is an orbital type and
M
is a model in
\mathfrak{K}
as follows:
pFM
if
p\in\mathscr{S}(N)
for some
N\geq_{\mathfrak{K}}M
and
p
does not fork over
M
. We let
\mathfrak{s}(\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits)\coloneqq(\mathfrak{K},F(\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits))
.

Properties of
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
generalize to
\overline{\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits}
as follows:

Fact 7.5

(
[
LRV
, 8.4,8.5]
)
.

Let
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
be a two-dimensional independence relation on
\mathfrak{K}
.

(1)

Let
M_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{\ell}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{3}
for
\ell=1,2
. Then
M_{1}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{M_{3}}M_{2}
if and only if
M_{1}\overset{M_{3}}{\underset{M_{0}}{\overline{\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits}}}M_{2}
.

(2)

(Preservation under
\mathfrak{K}
-embeddings) Given
M_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{3}
,
A,B\subseteq|M_{3}|
, and
f:M_{3}\rightarrow N_{3}
, we have that
A\overset{M_{3}}{\underset{M_{0}}{\overline{\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits}}}B
if and only if
f[A]\overset{N_{3}}{\underset{f[M_{0}]}{\overline{\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits}}}f[B]
.

(3)

(Monotonicity) If
A\overset{M_{3}}{\underset{M_{0}}{\overline{\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits}}}B
and
A_{0}\subseteq A
,
B_{0}\subseteq B
, then
A_{0}\overset{M_{3}}{\underset{M_{0}}{\overline{\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits}}}B_{0}
.

(4)

(Normality)
A\overset{M_{3}}{\underset{M_{0}}{\overline{\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits}}}B
if and only if
AM_{0}\overset{M_{3}}{\underset{M_{0}}{\overline{\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits}}}BM_{0}
.

(5)

(Base monotonicity) If
A\overset{M_{3}}{\underset{M_{0}}{\overline{\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits}}}B
,
M_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{2}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{3}
, and
|M_{2}|\subseteq B
, then
A\overset{M_{3}}{\underset{M_{2}}{\overline{\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits}}}B
.

(6)

(Extension) Whenever
M\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N
and
p\in\mathscr{S}^{<\infty}(M)
, there exists
q\in\mathscr{S}^{<\infty}(N)
extending
p
such that
q
does not fork over
M
.

(7)

(Symmetry)
A\overset{N}{\underset{M}{\overline{\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits}}}B
holds if and only if
B\overset{N}{\underset{M}{\overline{\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits}}}A
holds.

(8)

(Uniqueness) Given
p,q\in\mathscr{S}^{<\infty}(B;N)
with
M\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N
and
|M|\subseteq B\subseteq|N|
, if
p\,{\upharpoonright}\,M=q\,{\upharpoonright}\,M
and
p
,
q
do not fork over
M
, then
p=q
.

(9)

(Transitivity) If
M_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{2}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{3}
,
A\overset{M_{3}}{\underset{M_{0}}{\overline{\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits}}}M_{2}
and
A\overset{M_{3}}{\underset{M_{2}}{\overline{\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits}}}B
, then
A\overset{M_{3}}{\underset{M_{0}}{\overline{\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits}}}B
.

In a general two-dimensional independence relation
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
,
\mathfrak{s}(\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits)
may not induce a good frame (because e.g. such a relation also exists in strictly stable first-order theories). We call the ones that do (and satisfy a few more convenient properties)
good
:

Definition 7.6
.

A two-dimensional independence notion
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
on
\mathfrak{K}
is
good
if it satisfies the following properties:

(1)

\mathfrak{s}(\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits)
is a good frame on
\mathfrak{K}
(in particular,
\mathfrak{K}
is a fragmented AEC).

(2)

Long transitivity: if
\delta
is a limit ordinal,
\langle M_{i}:i\leq\delta\rangle
,
\langle N_{i}:i\leq\delta\rangle
are increasing continuous in
\mathfrak{K}
and
N_{i}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{i}}^{N_{i+1}}M_{i+1}
for all
i<\delta
, then
N_{0}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{N_{\delta}}M_{\delta}
.

(3)

Local character: if
M\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N
and
A\subseteq|N|
, there exists
M_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N_{0}
such that
N_{0}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{N}M
,
A\subseteq|N_{0}|
, and
\|N_{0}\|\leq|A|+\text{LS}(\mathfrak{K})
.

Note that the local character property of two-dimensional independence notions is vacuous in case the relation is on
\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}
. In this case, the following replacement is useful (this is related to the definition of successful
\text{good}^{+}
in
[
She09a
, III]
, see
[
BV
, 2.14]
; the “reflects down” terminology appears in
[
Vas17b
, 3.7(2)]
):

Definition 7.7
.

A good two-dimensional independence notion
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
on
\mathfrak{K}

reflects down
if for any
\lambda\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{K})
and any two
\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}
-increasing continuous chains
\langle M_{i}:i<\lambda^{+}\rangle
,
\langle N_{i}:i<\lambda^{+}\rangle
in
\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}
and
M_{i}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N_{i}
for
i<\lambda^{+}
, there is a club
C\subseteq\lambda^{+}
such that for any
i<j
in
C
,
N_{i}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{i}}^{N_{j}}M_{j}
(note that this implies
M_{i}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N_{i}
).

Remark 7.8
.

If
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
is a good two-dimensional independence notion on
\mathfrak{K}
and
\lambda,\lambda^{+}\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{K})
, then by local character
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}
reflects down.

We will use the following very useful fact about good two-dimensional independence notions: a union of independent limit squares is limit, in the following sense:

Fact 7.9

(
[
She09a
, II.6.29]
)
.

Let
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
be a good two-dimensional independence notion on
\mathfrak{K}
. Let
\langle M_{i}:i\leq\delta\rangle
,
\langle N_{i}:i\leq\delta\rangle
be increasing continuous in
\mathfrak{K}
such that
N_{i}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{i}}^{N_{i+1}}M_{i+1}
for all
i<\delta
. If
N_{i+1}
is limit over
M_{i}\cup N_{i}
(see Definition
3.5
(
4
)) for all
i<\delta
, then
N_{\delta}
is limit over
M_{\delta}\cup N_{0}
.

Regarding limit models, we also have that one can resolve them in a nice way:

Fact 7.10

(
[
She09a
, III.1.17]
)
.

Let
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
be a good two-dimensional independence notion on
\mathfrak{K}
. Let
\lambda\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{K})
be such that
\lambda^{+}\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{K})
. Let
M,N\in\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda^{+}}
be limit such that
N
is limit over
M
. Then there exists
\langle M_{i}:i<\lambda^{+}\rangle,\langle N_{i}:i<\lambda^{+}\rangle
increasing continuous resolutions of
M
and
N
in
\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}
such that
M_{i}
is limit and
N_{i}
is limit over
M_{i}
for all
i<\lambda^{+}
.

Being good and reflecting down is useful, but one sometimes wants more than the long transitivity property. This is the content of the next definition:

Definition 7.11
.

A two-dimensional independence notion
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
on
\mathfrak{K}
is
very good
if it is good, resolvable, reflects down and satisfies in addition
strong continuity
: whenever
\langle M_{\ell}^{i}:i\leq\delta\rangle
are increasing continuous in
\mathfrak{K}
,
\ell<4
, and
M_{1}^{i}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}^{i}}^{M_{3}^{i}}M_{2}^{i}
for all
i<\delta
, then
M_{1}^{\delta}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}^{\delta}}^{M_{3}^{\delta}}M_{2}^{\delta}
.

We now proceed to show that very good categorical two-dimensional independence notions are canonical. This is essentially
[
BGKV16
]
, but since the setup here is not as global as there, we use a slightly different road.

Lemma 7.12
.

If
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{1}
and
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{2}
are very good two-dimensional independence notions on
\mathfrak{K}
and
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{1}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}=\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{2}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}
for all
\lambda\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{K})
, then
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{1}=\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{2}
.

Proof.

Assume that
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{1}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},M_{3})
. We show that
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{2}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},M_{3})
, and the converse will be symmetric. We proceed by induction on
\|M_{3}\|
. If
\|M_{0}\|=\|M_{3}\|
, then by assumption
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{2}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},M_{3})
. Assume now that
\|M_{0}\|<\|M_{3}\|
. Let
\delta\coloneqq\|M_{3}\|
. For
\ell=1,2,3
, build
\langle M_{\ell}^{i}:i\leq\delta\rangle
increasing continuous such that for all
i\leq\delta
:

(1)

\|M_{\ell}^{i}\|=\|M_{0}\|+|i|
for
\ell=1,2,3
.

(2)

\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{1}(M_{0},M_{1}^{i},M_{2}^{i},M_{3}^{i}).

(3)

M_{\ell}^{\delta}=M_{\ell}
for
\ell=1,2,3
.

This is possible using monotonicity and the fact that
\mathfrak{K}
is a fragmented AEC. This is enough: by the induction hypothesis,
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{1}(M_{0},M_{1}^{i},M_{2}^{i},M_{3}^{i})
for all
i<\delta
. By strong continuity,
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{2}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},M_{3})
, as desired.
∎

Fact 7.13
.

Let
\mathfrak{K}
be fragmented AEC and let
\lambda\geq\text{LS}(\mathfrak{K})
. Let
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{1}
and
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{2}
be two good two-dimensional independence notions on
\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}
which reflect down. If
\mathfrak{K}
is categorical in
\lambda
, then
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{1}=\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{2}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{s}^{\ell}\coloneqq\mathfrak{s}(\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{\ell})
. By canonicity of categorical good frames (Fact
6.7
),
\mathfrak{s}^{1}=\mathfrak{s}^{2}
, so write
\mathfrak{s}\coloneqq\mathfrak{s}^{1}
. By
[
Vas17b
, 3.11]
,
\mathfrak{s}
has the existence property for uniqueness triples (see
[
She09a
, II.5.3(3)]
). By
[
She09a
, II.6.3(3)]
,
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{1}=\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{2}
.
∎

Theorem 7.14

(Canonicity)
.

If
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{1}
and
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{2}
are two very good two-dimensional independence notions on
\mathfrak{K}
and
\mathfrak{K}
is categorical in every
\lambda\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{K})
, then
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{1}=\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{2}
.

Proof.

By Fact
7.13
,
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{1}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}=\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits^{2}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}
for every
\lambda\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{K})
. Now apply Lemma
7.12
.
∎

The next topic is when (very) good two-dimensional independence notions exist. It is natural to construct them from good frames, since we already have sufficient conditions for the existence of good frames (Fact
6.2
). A good frame that can be extended to a good two-dimensional independence relation which reflects down will be called
extendible
. See
7.17
for another justification of the name.

Definition 7.15
.

We say a good frame
\mathfrak{s}
is
extendible
if there is a good two-dimensional independence notion
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
on
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{s}}
which reflects down. We say that
\mathfrak{s}
is
very good
if in addition we can find such an
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
which is also very good.

We will use the following sufficient condition for a good frame to be extendible:

Fact 7.16
.

Let
\mathfrak{s}
be a good frame and let
\lambda\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{s})
. If
2^{\lambda}<2^{\lambda^{+}}
,
\mathfrak{s}
is categorical in
\lambda
, and
\lambda^{+}\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{s})
, then
\mathfrak{s}_{\lambda}
is extendible.

Proof.

Since
\lambda^{+}\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{s}),
every model in
\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda^{+}}
has a universal model over it. Thus by
[
Vas17b
, E.8]
(the main idea of the argument is due to the first author, see
[
She09a
, p. 798]
),
\mathfrak{s}_{\lambda}
has what is called the existence property for uniqueness triples. By
[
She09a
, II.6.34]
, there is a two-dimensional independence relation
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
on
\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}
which satisfies long transitivity and respects
\mathfrak{s}_{\lambda}
. Now, any time a type does not
\mathfrak{s}(\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits)
-fork, this implies that it does not
\mathfrak{s}_{\lambda}
-fork as
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
respects
\mathfrak{s}_{\lambda}
. But we know that
\mathfrak{s}
satisfies uniqueness and
\mathfrak{s}(\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits)
satisfies extension, see Fact
7.5
, so by the proof of
[
BGKV16
, 4.1]
,
\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{s}(\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits)
. We have shown that
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
is good (since we only consider it as a relation on
\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}
, local character is not relevant).

It remains to see that
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
reflects down. Since
\mathfrak{s}
is a good frame and
\lambda^{+}\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{s})
, we have that
\left(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{s}}\right)_{[\lambda,\lambda^{+}]}
is
\lambda
-tame (Remark
6.3
). Thus we can apply
[
Jar16
, 7.15]
to obtain that
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
reflects down, as desired.
∎

Note also that being extendible implies that the frame itself can be extended (this is the main idea of the end of Chapter II in
[
She09a
]
):

Fact 7.17
.

If
\mathfrak{s}
is an extendible categorical good
\lambda
-frame, then there exists a (unique) categorical good frame
\mathfrak{t}
such that
\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{t})=\{\lambda,\lambda^{+}\}
and
\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda}=\mathfrak{s}_{\lambda}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{K}
be the AEC generated by
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{s}}
. By
[
She09a
, III.1.6(2)]
(or see
[
JS13
, 10.1.9]
), there is a good
\lambda^{+}
-frame
\mathfrak{s}^{+}
on
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{sat}}_{\lambda^{+}}
. Since by
[
She09a
, III.1.10]
,
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{sat}}_{\lambda^{+}}
is
\lambda
-tame, nonforking in
\mathfrak{s}^{+}
must be generated by nonforking in
\mathfrak{s}
(i.e.
\mathfrak{s}^{+}=\mathfrak{s}^{up}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathfrak{K}^{\text{sat}}_{\lambda^{+}}
). Thus letting
\mathfrak{t}\coloneqq\mathfrak{s}^{up}\,{\upharpoonright}\,(\mathfrak{K}^{\text{sat}}_{\lambda^{+}}\cup\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda})
, we get the result.
∎

Definition 7.18
.

For
\mathfrak{s}
an extendible categorical good
\lambda
-frame, we write
\mathfrak{s}^{+}
for
\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda^{+}}
, where
\mathfrak{t}
is as given by Fact
7.17
.

Since we now know how to extend a frame to the next cardinal, it makes sense to define when one can do this successively:

Definition 7.19
.

[
[
She09a
, III.1.12]
]

Let
\mathfrak{s}
be a categorical good
\lambda
-frame. We define by induction on
n<\omega
what it means for
\mathfrak{s}
to be
n
-extendible as well as a good
\lambda^{+n}
-frame
\mathfrak{s}^{+n}
as follows:

(1)

\mathfrak{s}
is always
0
-extendible and
\mathfrak{s}^{+0}=\mathfrak{s}
.

(2)

\mathfrak{s}
is
(n+1)
-extendible if it is
n
-extendible and
\mathfrak{s}^{+n}
is extendible.

(3)

If
\mathfrak{s}
is
(n+1)
-extendible, let
\mathfrak{s}^{+(n+1)}\coloneqq\left(\mathfrak{s}^{+n}\right)^{+}
.

We say that
\mathfrak{s}
is
(<\omega)
-extendible
if
\mathfrak{s}
is
n
-extendible for all
n<\omega
.

When a good
\lambda
-frame is
(<\omega)
-extendible, then after taking its successor a few times, it becomes very good. Moreover after this is done one can “connect” forking between the cardinals, getting a good
[\lambda,\lambda^{<\omega})
-frame:

Fact 7.20
.

Let
\mathfrak{s}
be a categorical good
\lambda
-frame.

(1)

If
\mathfrak{s}
is
\omega
-successful and
\text{good}^{+}
(in the sense of
[
She09a
, III.1]
), then
\mathfrak{s}
is
(<\omega)
-extendible.

(2)

If
\mathfrak{s}
is
4
-extendible, then
\mathfrak{s}^{+3}
is very good.

(3)

If
\mathfrak{s}
is very good and
2
-extendible, then
\mathfrak{s}^{+}
is very good.

(4)

If
\mathfrak{s}
is very good and
(<\omega)
-extendible, then there exists a (unique) very good categorical frame
\mathfrak{t}
such that
\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{t})=[\lambda,\lambda^{+\omega})
and
\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda^{+n}}=\mathfrak{s}^{+n}
for all
n<\omega
.

Proof.

(1)

Essentially follows from
[
BV
, 2.14]
.

(2)

By
[
She09a
, III.8.19]
(see the proof of
[
Vas16a
, 12.14]
). The four levels of extendibility are used to get a “better” frame each time we go from
\mathfrak{s}^{+k}
to
\mathfrak{s}^{+(k+1)}.
Strong continuity is the hard part to obtain in this process, and Shelah‘s proof uses quite sophisticated orthogonality calculus.

(3)

Also by
[
She09a
, III.8.19]
.

(4)

Let
\mathfrak{t}\coloneqq\mathfrak{s}^{up}\,{\upharpoonright}\,(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{s}}\cup\mathfrak{K}^{\text{sat}}_{[\lambda^{+},\lambda^{+\omega})})
. As in the proof of Fact
7.17
, we have enough tameness for nonforking to connect, so this works (see also
[
Vas17b
, Appendix A]
).

∎

While in general, it is not known how to build extendible good frames without using the weak diamond (Fact
7.16
), in the compact case it is possible:

Fact 7.21
.

Let
\mathfrak{K}
be a compact AEC. If
\mathfrak{K}
is categorical in some
\mu>\text{LS}(\mathfrak{K})
, then there exists a (unique) categorical very good frame on
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{sat}}_{\geq\text{LS}(\mathfrak{K})^{+6}}
.

Proof.

Let
\kappa
be such that
\mathfrak{K}
is
\kappa
-compact. By Corollary
4.8
,
\mathfrak{K}
has amalgamation, no maximal models, is
(<\kappa)
-short, and is categorical in a proper class of cardinals. By essentially the main result of
[
Vas16a
]
, and more precisely by
[
Vas17d
, A.16]
,
{\mathfrak{K}}^{\lambda\text{-sat}}
is what is called there fully good. Here, we have set
\lambda\coloneqq\left(\text{LS}(\mathfrak{K})^{<\kappa}\right)^{+5}
. Since
\kappa
is strongly compact, we have by a result of Solovay (see the proof of
[
Jec03
, 20.8]
) that
\text{LS}(\mathfrak{K})^{<\kappa}\leq\text{LS}(\mathfrak{K})^{+}
(of course this also holds if
\kappa=\aleph_{0}
). Thus
\lambda\leq\text{LS}(\mathfrak{K})^{+6}
. Checking the definition of fully good in
[
Vas16a
, 8.4]
, we see that this implies that
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{sat}}_{\geq\text{LS}(\mathfrak{K})^{+6}}
carries a very good two-dimensional independence relation, as desired.
∎

8.
Multidimensional independence

We define here the main notion of this paper: multidimensional independence relations. This section contains mostly definitions and easy lemmas. A result of importance is how to build multidimensional independence relations from the two-dimensional relations (Theorem
8.20
).

8.1.
Systems

We start by defining what is meant by a system of models.

Definition 8.1
.

For
\mathfrak{K}
an abstract class and
I=(I,\leq)
a partial order, an
(I,\mathfrak{K})
-system
is a sequence
\mathbf{m}=\langle M_{u}:u\in I\rangle
such that
u\leq v
implies
M_{u}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{v}
. A
\mathfrak{K}
-system
is an
(I,\mathfrak{K})
-system for some
I
. For
\mathbf{m}
a
\mathfrak{K}
-system, we write
I(\mathbf{m})
for the unique
I
such that
\mathbf{m}
is an
(I,\mathfrak{K})
-system. When
\mathfrak{K}
is clear from context, we omit it from all the above definitions.

In practice,
I
is quite often
\mathcal{P}^{-}(n),
or
\mathcal{P}(n).
Nevertheless to easily carry out induction in the study of the combinatorial properties of these systems, we need to consider general orders.

We will often be interested in systems where all extensions are strict. In fact, a strengthening of this, being proper, is more useful (one can see it as an abstract version of Definition
3.5
(
4
), when we take
A
to consist of the union of several models):

Definition 8.2
.

Let
\mathbf{m}=\langle M_{u}:u\in I\rangle
be an
I
-system. Let
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
be a skeleton of
\mathfrak{K}
(the reader can think of
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}=\mathfrak{K}
at first reading but the interesting case is
K^{*},
the class of saturated models, in a good frame and
M<_{\mathfrak{K}^{*}}N
iff
M\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N
and
N
limit over M).

(1)

Let
u\in I
. We say that
u
is
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
-proper in
\mathbf{m}
if
M_{u}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
and there exists
N\in\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
such that:

(a)

For all
v\in I
,
v<u
implies
M_{v}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N<_{\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}}M_{u}
(note that the last inequality is strict!).

(b)

For all
v\in I
, if
v<u
and
M_{v}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
, then
M_{v}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}}N
.

(2)

For
I_{0}
a sub-order of
I
, we say that
\mathbf{m}
is
(I_{0},\mathfrak{K}^{\ast})
-proper
if every
u\in I_{0}
is
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
-proper in
\mathbf{m}
. When
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}=\mathfrak{K},
we omit it and when
I_{0}=I
we may omit it.

Note that a system where all extensions are strict may not be proper:

Example 8.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{K}
be the AEC of all infinite sets, ordered by subset. Let
A\subseteq B
be two countably infinite sets with
B\backslash A
infinite. Partition
B\backslash A
into two non-empty sets
C_{1}
and
C_{2},

C_{1}
finite so
C_{2}
infinite and let
A_{\ell}\coloneqq A\cup C_{\ell}
. Then the
\mathcal{P}(2)
-system
(A,A_{1},A_{2},B)
is not proper but all the extensions are strict.

The next two lemmas are easy properties of proper systems: being proper does not depend on the exact indexing set, and proper systems can be built.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
I_{0}\subseteq I_{1}\subseteq I
all be partial orders and let
\mathbf{m}
be an
I
-system. Let
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
be a skeleton of
\mathfrak{K}
. If
\mathbf{m}
is
(I_{0},\mathfrak{K}^{\ast})
-proper, then
\mathbf{m}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I_{1}
is
(I_{0},\mathfrak{K}^{\ast})
-proper.

Proof.

Straightforward.
∎

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
be a skeleton of
\mathfrak{K}
(Definition
3.8
). Let
\mathbf{m}=\langle M_{u}:u\in I\rangle
be an
I
-system with
I
a finite partial order and let
v
be maximal in
I
. If
\mathfrak{K}
has no maximal models, then there exists
N\in\mathfrak{K}
such that
M_{v}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N
and
v
is
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
-proper in
\mathbf{m}\,{\upharpoonright}\,(I\backslash\{v\})\smallfrown\langle N\rangle
(restriction of a system means what is expected and is defined right after this lemma).

Proof.

Using that
\mathfrak{K}
has no maximal models, let
M\in\mathfrak{K}
be such that
M_{v}<_{\mathfrak{K}}M
. Now apply Lemma
3.9
with
M
,
\langle M_{i}:i<n\rangle
there standing for
M
,
\{M_{u}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}\mid u<v\}
here.
∎

It is now time to define how systems can relate to each other, in particular how they can be isomorphic, extensions, etc.

Definition 8.6
.

(1)

Let
I\subseteq J
be partial orders and let
\mathbf{m}=\langle M_{u}:u\in J\rangle
be a
J
-system. Then
\mathbf{m}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I\coloneqq\langle M_{u}:u\in I\rangle
.

(2)

For
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}=\langle M_{u}^{\ell}:u\in I\rangle
,
\ell=1,2
both
I
-systems, say
\mathbf{m}_{1}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}\mathbf{m}_{2}
if
M_{u}^{1}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{u}^{2}
for all
u\in I
. We say that
\mathbf{m}_{2}
is a
disjoint
extension of
\mathbf{m}_{1}
(written
\mathbf{m}_{1}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}^{d}\mathbf{m}_{2}
) if
\mathbf{m}_{1}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}\mathbf{m}_{2}
and
M_{u}^{2}\cap M_{v}^{1}\subseteq M_{u}^{1}
when
u\leq_{I}v
.

(3)

[
She09a
, III.12.14(4)]
For
k<\omega
,
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}=\langle M_{u}^{\ell}:u\in I\rangle
,
\ell<k
all
I
-systems with
\mathbf{m}_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}\mathbf{m}_{1}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}\ldots\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}\mathbf{m}_{k-1}
, let
\mathbf{m}\coloneqq\mathbf{m}_{0}\ast\mathbf{m}_{1}\ast\ldots\ast\mathbf{m}_{k-1}
be the system defined as follows:

(a)

It is indexed by the partial order
J\coloneqq I\times k
ordered lexicographically, so
\mathbf{m}=\langle M_{u}:u\in J\rangle
.

(b)

For all
u\in I
and
i<k
,
M_{(u,i)}=M_{u}^{i}
.

(4)

For
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}=\langle M_{u}^{\ell}:u\in I\rangle
,
\ell=1,2
both
I
-systems, we say
f
is a
system embedding
from
\mathbf{m}_{1}
to
\mathbf{m}_{2}
and write
f:\mathbf{m}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbf{m}_{2}
if
f=\langle f_{u}:u\in I\rangle
, for all
u\in I
,
f_{u}
is a
\mathfrak{K}
-embedding from
M_{u}^{1}
to
M_{u}^{2}
, and
u\leq v
implies
f_{u}\subseteq f_{v}
.

(5)

For
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}=\langle M_{u}^{\ell}:u\in I\rangle
,
\ell=1,2
both
I
-systems, we say
f
is an
isomorphism
from
\mathbf{m}_{1}
to
\mathbf{m}_{2}
and write
f:\mathbf{m}_{1}\cong\mathbf{m}_{2}
if
f=\langle f_{u}:u\in I\rangle
is a system embedding from
\mathbf{m}_{1}
to
\mathbf{m}_{2}
and for all
u\in I
,
f_{u}
is an isomorphism from
M_{u}^{1}
onto
M_{u}^{2}
.

(6)

For
\pi:I_{1}\cong I_{2}
an isomorphism of partial orders,
\mathbf{m}=\langle M_{u}:u\in I_{1}\rangle
an
I_{1}
-system, let
\pi(\mathbf{m})
denote the
I_{2}
-system
\langle M_{\pi^{-1}(v)}:v\in I_{2}\rangle
.

(7)

A system
\mathbf{m}=\langle M_{u}:u\in I\rangle
is called
disjoint
if whenever
u,v,w,u^{\ast}\in I
are such that
u\leq v\leq u^{\ast}
and
u\leq w\leq u^{\ast}
and
v\cap w=u,
then
M_{u}=M_{v}\cap M_{w}
.

(8)

A system
\mathbf{m}=\langle M_{u}:u\in I\rangle
is called
fully disjoint
if whenever
u,v,w\in I
are such that
u\leq v,v\cap w=u
and
u\leq w
, then
M_{u}=M_{v}\cap M_{w}
.

Remark 8.7
.

Every disjoint system is isomorphic to a fully disjoint system.

It is often useful to code systems as an abstract class:

Definition 8.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{K}
be an abstract class and let
I
be a partial order. The
vocabulary of
(\mathfrak{K},I)
-systems
is
\tau^{I}\coloneqq\tau(\mathfrak{K})\cup\{P_{i}:i\in I\}
, where each
P_{i}
is a new unary predicate. The
abstract class of
(\mathfrak{K},I)
-systems
is the abstract class
\mathfrak{K}^{I}=(K^{I},\leq_{\mathfrak{K}^{I}})
defined as follows:

(1)

K^{I}
consists of all the
\tau^{I}
-structures
M
such that
\langle M^{P_{i}}:i\in I\rangle
forms a fully disjoint
(\mathfrak{K},I)
-system (we see
M^{P_{i}}
as a
\tau(\mathfrak{K})
-structure). We identify the elements of
K^{I}
with the corresponding systems.

(2)

\mathbf{m}_{1}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}^{I}}\mathbf{m}_{2}
if and only if
\mathbf{m}_{1}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}^{d}\mathbf{m}_{2}
(see Definition
8.6
(
2
)).

8.2.
Multidimensional independence relations

Multidimensional independence relations consist of systems indexed by a certain class of semilattices. For reasons that will become apparent, we require that this class has a certain amount of closure:

Definition 8.9
.

A class
\mathcal{I}
of semilattices is called
closed
if:

(A)

\mathcal{I}
is closed under isomorphisms.

(B)

\mathcal{I}
is closed under taking initial segments.

(C)

For any
I\in\mathcal{I}
and any
u\in I\backslash\{\bot\}
,
[u,\infty)_{I}\times\{0,1\}\in\mathcal{I}
.

(D)

(Follows) If
I_{0}\times\{0,1\}\in\mathcal{I},
then
I_{0}\in\mathcal{I}
(it is isomorphic to an initial segment of
\mathcal{I}_{0}\times\{0,1\}
).

The main examples of a closed class of semilattice are:

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
n<\omega
and let
\mathcal{I}
be the class of all initial semilattices isomorphic to an initial segments of
\mathcal{P}(n)
. Then
\mathcal{I}
is closed.

Proof.

The third condition of Definition
8.9
is the only non-trivial one. Let
I\in\mathcal{I}
, and identify it with an initial segment of
\mathcal{P}(n)
. Let
u\in I\backslash\{\bot\}
(so
u\neq\emptyset
). Then
[u,\infty)_{I}
is the set of all subsets of
\mathcal{P}(n)
extending
u
. As a semilattice, it is isomorphic to
\mathcal{P}(n\backslash u)\cong\mathcal{P}(n-|u|)
. Thus
[u,\infty)_{I}\times\{0,1\}\cong\mathcal{P}(n-|u|)\times\mathcal{P}(1)\cong\mathcal{P}(n-|u|+1)
, which since
|u|\geq 1
is an initial segment of
\mathcal{P}(n)
.
∎

A multidimensional independence relation consists of an abstract class
\mathfrak{K}
, a closed class of finite semilattices, and a class of systems indexed by these semilattices. Several properties are required, akin to the requirements in the definition of a two-dimensional independence relation. A complication arises in the Monotonicity properties: there are several and may not always seem natural (it may help to jump ahead and come back once the properties are used; for example a good hint to understand the definition of Monotonocity
4
is to looks at the proof of Lemma
8.13
). Nevertheless they will hold for the relation we construct and are all used in the next section. Note that we do not know whether a multidimensional independence relations are canonical (i.e. whether under reasonable conditions, also involving the existence and uniqueness properties to be defined later, there can be at most one satisfying this list of axiom).

Definition 8.11
.

A
multidimensional independence relation (or notion)
is a triple
\mathfrak{i}=(\mathfrak{K},\mathcal{I},\operatorname{NF})
, where
\mathfrak{K}=\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}
is an abstract class,
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}}
is a class of semilattices, and every element of
\operatorname{NF}=\operatorname{NF}_{\mathfrak{i}}
is a
(\mathfrak{K},I)
-systems with
I\in\mathcal{I}.
We call the members of
\operatorname{NF}

independent systems
, and write
\operatorname{NF}(\mathbf{m})
instead of
\mathbf{m}\in\operatorname{NF}.
We require the following properties:

(1)

\mathcal{I}
is a
closed
class of
finite
semilattices.

(2)

Invariance: If
\mathbf{m}
and
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
are systems and
f:\mathbf{m}\cong\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
, then
\operatorname{NF}(\mathbf{m})
if and only if
\operatorname{NF}(\mathbf{m}^{\prime})
.

(3)

Nontriviality:
\operatorname{NF}\neq\emptyset
and for every
I\in\mathcal{I}
and every
M\in\mathfrak{K}
, there exists an independent
I
-system containing
M
.

(4)

Disjointness: Every independent system is disjoint (see Definition
8.6
(
7
)).

(5)

Symmetry: If
I_{1},I_{2}\in\mathcal{I}
,
\pi:I_{1}\cong I_{2}
is an isomorphism, and
\mathbf{m}
is an
I_{1}
-system, then
\operatorname{NF}(\mathbf{m})
if and only if
\operatorname{NF}(\pi(\mathbf{m}))
, where
\pi(\mathbf{m})
is the
I_{2}
-system naturally induced by
\pi
(see Definition
8.6
(
6
)).

(6)

Transitivity: If
\mathbf{m}_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}\mathbf{m}_{1}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}\mathbf{m}_{2}
,
\mathbf{m}_{0}\ast\mathbf{m}_{1}
is independent, and
\mathbf{m}_{1}\ast\mathbf{m}_{2}
is independent, then
\mathbf{m}_{0}\ast\mathbf{m}_{2}
is independent.

(7)

Monotonicity 1: If
\mathbf{m}
is an
I
-system,
\operatorname{NF}(\mathbf{m})
, and
J\subseteq I
is such that
J\in\mathcal{I}
, then
\operatorname{NF}(\mathbf{m}\,{\upharpoonright}\,J)
.

(8)

Monotonicity 2: If
\mathbf{m}=\langle M_{u}:u\in I\rangle
is an
I
-system,
v
is a maximal element in
I
, and
M_{v}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N
, then
\mathbf{m}
is independent if and only if
\langle M_{u}:u\in I\backslash\{v\}\rangle\smallfrown\langle N\rangle
is independent.

(9)

Monotonicity 3: If
\mathbf{m}
is an
I
-system, then
\mathbf{m}
is independent if and only if for any maximal
v\in I
,
\mathbf{m}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\{u\in I:u\leq v\}
is independent.

(10)

Monotonicity 4: Let
I_{1},I_{2}\subseteq I
all be in
\mathcal{I}
. Let
\mathbf{m}
be an
I
-system. If:

(a)

I_{1}
is an initial segment of
I
.

(b)

I_{1}\cap I_{2}
is cofinal in
I_{1}
.

(c)

I_{1}\cup I_{2}=I
.

(d)

\mathbf{m}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I_{1}
and
\mathbf{m}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I_{2}
are independent.

Then
\mathbf{m}
is independent.

(11)

Monotonicity 5: Let
I\in\mathcal{I}
, let
u\in I\backslash\{\bot\}
, and let
I_{0}\coloneqq[u,\infty)_{I}
. Let
\mathbf{m}
,
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
be
I
-systems,
\mathbf{m}_{0}\coloneqq\mathbf{m}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I_{0}
be independent, and
\mathbf{m}_{0}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I_{0}
be such that
\mathbf{m}_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}\mathbf{m}_{0}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{m}_{0}\ast\mathbf{m}_{0}^{\prime}
is independent. Assume that
\mathbf{m}\,{\upharpoonright}\,(I\backslash I_{0})=\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,(I\backslash I_{0})
. Then
\mathbf{m}
is independent if and only if
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
is independent.

The natural restrictions can be defined on a multidimensional independence relation:

Definition 8.12
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}=(\mathfrak{K},\mathcal{I},\operatorname{NF})
be a multidimensional independence relation.

(1)

For
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{I}
a class of partial orders, we let
\mathfrak{i}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{J}
denote the multidimensional independence relation
\mathfrak{j}=(\mathfrak{K},\mathcal{J},\operatorname{NF}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{J})
, where
\operatorname{NF}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{J}
denotes the class of systems in
\operatorname{NF}
that are
J
-systems for
J\in\mathcal{J}
.

(2)

For
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
a sub-abstract class of
\mathfrak{K}
, let
\mathfrak{i}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
denote the multidimensional independence relation
\mathfrak{j}=(\mathfrak{K}^{\ast},\mathcal{I},\operatorname{NF}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathfrak{K}^{\ast})
, where
\operatorname{NF}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
denotes the class of systems in
\operatorname{NF}
that are also
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
-systems. We may write
\mathfrak{i}_{\lambda}
instead of
\mathfrak{i}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}
.

The next lemma shows that, under some closure conditions on
\mathcal{I}
, transitivity follows from the other axioms:

Lemma 8.13
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}
be a multidimensional independence relation except transitivity is not assumed. Let
\mathbf{m}_{0},\mathbf{m}_{1},\mathbf{m}_{2}
be
I
-systems with
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}}
. If
\mathbf{m}_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}}\mathbf{m}_{1}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}}\mathbf{m}_{2}
,
\mathbf{m}_{0}\ast\mathbf{m}_{1}
is independent,
\mathbf{m}_{1}\ast\mathbf{m}_{2}
is independent, and
I\times 3\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}}
, then
\mathbf{m}_{0}\ast\mathbf{m}_{1}\ast\mathbf{m}_{2}
is independent. In particular, if
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}}
is closed under products the transitivity axiom follows from the others.

Proof.

Say the
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}
’s are
I
-systems. Let
J\coloneqq I\times 3
. Recalling Definition
8.6
(3) let
\mathbf{m}\coloneqq(\mathbf{m}_{0}\ast\mathbf{m}_{1}\ast\mathbf{m}_{2})
. We check that
\mathbf{m}
is independent by using monotonicity 4: let
I_{1}\coloneqq I\times\{0,1\}
and let
I_{2}\coloneqq I\times\{1,2\}
. We have that
\mathbf{m}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I_{1}\ast\mathbf{m}_{1}
and
\mathbf{m}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I_{2}=\mathbf{m}_{1}\ast\mathbf{m}_{2}.
Both are independent by assumption. Moreover,
I_{1}\cup I_{2}=J
,
I_{1}
is an initial segment of
J
, and
I_{1}\cap I_{2}=I\times\{1\}
is cofinal in
I_{1}
. Thus monotonicity 4 implies that
\mathbf{m}
is independent. The “in particular” part follows from monotonicity 1.
∎

One wants to order systems so that if
\mathbf{m}_{2}
extends
\mathbf{m}_{1}
, then the entire diagram that this forms is independent. This is formalized by looking at
\mathbf{m}_{1}\ast\mathbf{m}_{2}
:

Definition 8.14
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}
be a multidimensional independence relation. We define a binary relation
\leq_{\mathfrak{i}}
on
\operatorname{NF}_{\mathfrak{i}}
as follows:
\mathbf{m}_{1}\leq_{\mathfrak{i}}\mathbf{m}_{2}
if and only if
\mathbf{m}_{1}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}\mathbf{m}_{2}
(so in particular
I(\mathbf{m}_{1})=I(\mathbf{m}_{2})
) and
\operatorname{NF}_{\mathfrak{i}}(\mathbf{m}_{1}\ast\mathbf{m}_{2})
. Let
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i},I}
be the sub-abstract class of
\mathfrak{K}^{I}
(Definition
8.8
) consisting of all independent
I
-systems and ordered by
\leq_{\mathfrak{i}}
.

One may want in addition to require that the system
\mathbf{m}_{1}\ast\mathbf{m}_{2}
is
proper
:

Definition 8.15
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}
be a multidimensional independence relation. Let
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}}
. We let
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},I}
be the class of
proper
independent
I
-systems ordered by
\mathbf{m}_{1}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},I}}\mathbf{m}_{2}
if and only if either
\mathbf{m}_{1}=\mathbf{m}_{2}
or
\mathbf{m}_{1}\ast\mathbf{m}_{2}
is a
proper
independent system.

Absent from the properties in Definition
8.11
were any kind of extension or uniqueness. We define these now. Note that existence says there
is
a system, extension that an
existing
system can be extended. Strong uniqueness will hold only in classes of limit models, where also the extensions are limit: it says in particular that there is only one proper system indexed by a given lattice. Uniqueness is the property that will hold in the original class: it says that each independent system has at most one completion, in the sense that any two completions amalgamate (so this can apply only when we fix the cardinality).

Definition 8.16
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}=(\mathfrak{K},\mathcal{I},\operatorname{NF})
be a multidimensional independence relation. The following are additional properties that
\mathfrak{i}
may have:

(1)

Existence: For any
I\in\mathcal{I}
there exists a proper independent
I
-system.

(2)

Extension: For any
I,J\in\mathcal{I}
with
I
an initial segment of
J
, if
\mathbf{m}
is an independent
I
-system, then there exists a
(J\backslash I)
-proper independent
J
-system
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbf{m}\cong\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I
.

(3)

Strong uniqueness: Let
\mathbf{m}_{1}
,
\mathbf{m}_{2}
be independent
J
-systems and let
I
be an initial segment of
J
with
I\in\mathcal{I}
. Let
f:\mathbf{m}_{1}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I\cong\mathbf{m}_{2}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I
. If
\mathbf{m}_{1}
and
\mathbf{m}_{2}
are
(J\backslash I)
-proper (Definition
8.2
), then there exists
g:\mathbf{m}_{1}\cong\mathbf{m}_{2}
extending
f
.

(4)

Uniqueness: Let
\mathbf{m}_{1}
,
\mathbf{m}_{2}
be independent
J
-systems and let
I
be an initial segment of
J
with
J=I\cup\{v\}
,
I<v
. Let
f:\mathbf{m}_{1}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I\cong\mathbf{m}_{2}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I
. Then there exists an independent
J
-system
\mathbf{m}
and systems embeddings (see Definition
8.6
(
4
)
g_{\ell}\colon\mathbf{m}_{\ell}\rightarrow\mathbf{m}
,
\ell=1,2
such that
g_{\ell}
extends
f
and
g_{2}
extends
f^{-1}.

Any reasonable multidimensional independence relation induces a two-dimensional independence relation:

Definition 8.17
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}
be a multidimensional independence relation with
\mathcal{P}(2)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}}
. Define a
4
-ary relation
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits=\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits(\mathfrak{i})
by
M_{1}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{M_{3}}M_{2}
if and only if
(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},M_{3})
(seen as a system indexed by
\mathcal{P}(2)
) is an independent system.

Note that
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits(\mathfrak{i})
is morally the same as
\mathfrak{i}
restricted to
\mathcal{P}(2)
but they are different for boring reasons (the first is basically a
4
-ary on
\mathfrak{K}
, the second is basically a map from
\mathcal{P}(2)
to
\mathfrak{K}
).

Lemma 8.18
.

If
\mathfrak{i}
is a multidimensional independence relation with existence, extension, and uniqueness such that
\mathcal{P}(2)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}}
, then
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits(\mathfrak{i})
(from Definition
8.17
) is a two-dimensional independence notion on
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}
.

Proof.

The least trivial axioms to prove are monotonicity and symmetry. Symmetry can be obtained by using the symmetry property of multidimensional independence relations with the automorphism of
\mathcal{P}(2)
swapping
\{0\}
and
\{1\}
. Monotonicity actually follows from the other axioms, see
[
LRV
, 3.23]
.
∎

8.3.
Building a multidimensional independence relation

Starting from a two-dimensional independence relation, one can also build a multidimensional independence relation in the natural way:

Definition 8.19
.

(1) Let
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
be a two-dimensional independence notion on an abstract class
\mathfrak{K}
. Let
\mathfrak{i}=\mathfrak{i}({\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits})
be the following multidimensional independence relation:

(A)

\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}=\mathfrak{K}
.

(B)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}}
is the class of all finite semilattices.

(C)

For
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}}
and
\mathbf{m}=\langle M_{u}:u\in I\rangle
an
I
-system,
\mathbf{m}\in\operatorname{NF}_{\mathfrak{i}}
if and only if whenever
u_{1},u_{2},v\in I
and
u_{1}\leq v
,
u_{2}\leq v
, then
M_{u_{1}}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{u_{1}\land u_{2}}}^{M_{v}}M_{u_{2}}
.

(2) We say
\mathfrak{K}
has
uniqueness

(apply mainly to
\mathfrak{K}^{*}
above)
when
if
M<_{\mathfrak{K}^{*}}N_{\ell}
both have the same cardinality for
\ell=1,2
then
N_{1},N_{2}
are isomorphic over
M
(so
K^{*}
just
10
10

10

here
\mathfrak{K}^{*}=\mathfrak{K}
is not a reasonable choice as in the usual case,
\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}^{*}
cannot be an AEC.
an abstract class). The default
11
11

11

Earlier it was suggested
\mathfrak{K}^{*}=\mathfrak{K};
but for uniqueness to make sense we need this e.g.
8.20
case is
K^{*}
the class of saturated models, and
M<_{\mathfrak{K}^{*}}N
iff
M\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N
and
N
limit over
M.

Theorem 8.20
.

If
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
is a two-dimensional independence notion on an abstract class
\mathfrak{K}
with no maximal models, then
\mathfrak{i}=\mathfrak{i}(\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits)
is a multidimensional independence notion such that
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits(\mathfrak{i})=\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
. Moreover,
\mathfrak{i}
restricted to the class of initial segments of
\mathcal{P}(2)
has existence, extension, and uniqueness.

Proof.

Most of the axioms follow directly from the definitions. We only prove:

•

Monotonicity 4
: Let
I_{1},I_{2}
,
\mathbf{m}=\langle M_{u}:u\in I\rangle
be as in the statement of monotonicity 4. Let
u_{1},u_{2},v\in I
with
u_{1}\leq v
,
u_{2}\leq v
. We have to see that
M_{u_{1}}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{u_{1}\land u_{2}}}^{M_{v}}M_{u_{2}}
. If both
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are in
I_{1}
, or both are in
I_{2}
, then this is immediate from the assumptions that
\mathbf{m}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I_{1}
and
\mathbf{m}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I_{2}
are both independent (and preservation of
