\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
under
\mathfrak{K}
-embeddings). Assume now that one is in
I_{1}\backslash I_{2}
and the other in
I_{2}\backslash I_{1}
. By symmetry, without loss of generality
u_{1}\in I_{1}\backslash I_{2}
,
u_{2}\in I_{2}\backslash I_{1}
.

By assumption,
I_{1}\cap I_{2}
is maximal in
I
and
I=I_{1}\cup I_{2}
, so there exists
u^{\prime}\in I_{2}
such that
u_{1}\leq u^{\prime}
. Let
u_{1}^{\prime}\coloneqq\land\{u^{\prime}\in I_{2}\cap I_{1}\mid u_{1}\leq u^{\prime}\}
. In other words,
u_{1}^{\prime}
is the minimal element of
I_{1}\cap I_{2}
such that
u_{1}\leq u_{1}^{\prime}
. By minimality, we must have that
u_{1}^{\prime}\leq v
(otherwise, consider
u_{1}^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq u_{1}^{\prime}\land v
; since
I_{1}
is an initial segment, this is in
I_{1}\land I_{2}
and is still above
u_{1}
). By the previous paragraph, we have that
M_{u_{1}^{\prime}}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{u_{1}^{\prime}\land u_{2}}}^{M_{v}}M_{u_{2}}
. Also by the previous paragraph (recalling that
I_{1}
is an initial segment, so
u_{1}^{\prime}\land u_{2}\in I_{1}\cap I_{2}
),
M_{u_{1}}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{u_{1}\land u_{1}^{\prime}\land u_{2}}}^{M_{u_{1}^{\prime}}}M_{u_{1}^{\prime}\land u_{2}}
. By transitivity for
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
,
M_{u_{1}}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{u_{1}\land u_{1}^{\prime}\land u_{2}}}^{M_{v}}M_{u_{2}}
. Now note that
u_{1}\leq u_{1}^{\prime}
by assumption, hence
u_{1}\land u_{1}^{\prime}\land u_{2}=u_{1}\land u_{2}
, as desired.

•

Monotonicity 5
: Fix the data given by the statement of monotonicity 5. Write
\mathbf{m}=\langle M_{u}:u\in I\rangle
and
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}=\langle M_{u}^{\prime}:u\in I\rangle
.

–

We first show that
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
independent implies that
\mathbf{m}
is independent. Let
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\in I
be such that
u_{1},u_{2}\leq u_{3}
. We want to see that
M_{u_{1}}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{u_{1}\land u_{2}}}^{M_{u_{3}}}M_{u_{2}}
. There are several cases:

*

If
u_{1},u_{2}\notin I_{0}
, then
u_{1}\land u_{2}\notin I_{0}
(as
I\backslash I_{0}\subseteq I
), hence the result follows directly from the assumption that
M_{u_{1}}^{\prime}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{u_{1}\land u_{2}}^{\prime}}^{M_{u_{3}}^{\prime}}M_{u_{2}}^{\prime}
and ambient monotonicity.

*

If
u_{1},u_{2}\in I_{0}
, then apply transitivity.

*

If
u_{1}\in I_{0}
,
u_{2}\notin I_{0}
, and
u_{1}\land u_{2}\in I_{0}
, apply transitivity again.

*

If
u_{1}\in I_{0}
,
u_{2}\notin I_{0}
, and
u_{1}\land u_{2}\notin I_{0}
, use monotonicity.

–

Assume now that
\mathbf{m}
is independent. Let
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\in I
be such that
u_{1},u_{2}\leq u_{3}
. We want to see that
M_{u_{1}}^{\prime}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{u_{1}\land u_{2}}^{\prime}}^{M_{u_{3}}^{\prime}}M_{u_{2}}^{\prime}
and we again split into cases. The cases
u_{1},u_{2}\notin I_{0}
and
u_{1},u_{2}\in I_{0}
are similar to before. If
u_{1}\in I_{0}
,
u_{2}\notin I_{0}
, then
u_{3}\in I_{0}
since
I_{0}
is and end segment. Thus we can apply transitivity and we are done.

•

Transitivity
: follows from Lemma
8.13
.

That
\mathfrak{i}
restricted to the class of initial segments of
\mathcal{P}(2)
has existence, extension, and uniqueness follows from the corresponding properties of
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
: we only have to note that a
\mathcal{P}(2)
-system
\langle M_{u}:u\in\mathcal{P}(2)\rangle
is independent if and only if
M_{\{0\}}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{\emptyset}}^{M_{2}}M_{\{1\}}
, by the symmetry axiom of
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
.
∎

9.
Finite combinatorics of multidimensional independence

In this section, we assume:

Hypothesis 9.1
.

\mathfrak{i}=(\mathfrak{K},\mathcal{I},\operatorname{NF})
is a multidimensional independence relation.

We consider properties of
\mathfrak{i}
that are finitary in nature. That is, they do not depend on any kind of closure under unions of chains. This allows us to work at a high level of generality (for example,
\mathfrak{K}
is just assumed to be an abstract class). A crucial question is whether properties (such as extension or uniqueness) that hold for limit systems (that is, for systems consisting of limit models, ordered by being “limit over”) will also hold for the not necessarily limit other systems. We prove general positive results in this direction here (Theorems
9.12
and
9.15
). The statements use the notion of a skeleton. This is also a place where we use that
\mathcal{I}
is closed (Definition
8.9
), as well as the monotonicity axioms in Definition
8.11
.

We start by proving a few easy consequences of the definition of a multidimensional independence relation. First,
\mathcal{P}(1)
-systems are trivial:

Lemma 9.2
.

M\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N
both be in
\mathfrak{K}
. If
\mathcal{P}(1)\in\mathcal{I}
, then
\langle M,N\rangle
is an independent
I
-system.

Proof.

By nontriviality, there is an independent
\mathcal{P}(1)
-system
\mathbf{m}
containing
M
. Write
\mathbf{m}=\langle M,N^{\prime}\rangle
. By monotonicity 2,
\langle M,M\rangle
is independent. By monotonicity 2 again,
\langle M,N\rangle
is independent.
∎

It is also easy to see that existence is weaker than extension:

Lemma 9.3
.

If
\mathfrak{i}
has extension, then
\mathfrak{i}
has existence.

Proof.

Let
I\in\mathcal{I}
. Start with the empty system and extend it to a proper independent
I
-system.
∎

Uniqueness is also weaker than strong uniqueness:

Lemma 9.4
.

If
\mathfrak{i}
has strong uniqueness and
\mathfrak{K}
has no maximal models, then
\mathfrak{i}
has uniqueness.

Proof.

Given two models
M_{1},M_{2}
to amalgamate over an independent system
\mathbf{m}
, take a strict extension
M_{\ell}^{\prime}
of
M_{\ell}
and use strong uniqueness to see that
M_{1}^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
M_{2}^{\prime}
over
\mathbf{m}
, hence
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
amalgamate over
\mathbf{m}
.
∎

9.1.
Skeletons of independence relations

It is natural to consider what happens to
\mathfrak{i}
when restricting it to a skeleton of
\mathfrak{K}
. We overload terminology and also call such restrictions
skeletons
of
\mathfrak{i}
:

Definition 9.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}
and
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
be independence relations. We say that
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
is a
skeleton
of
\mathfrak{i}
if:

(A)

\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}}
is a skeleton of
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}
.

(B)

\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}=\mathfrak{i}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}}
.

Remark 9.6
.

If
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
is a skeleton of
\mathfrak{K}
, then it is straightforward to check that
\mathfrak{i}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
will be a multidimensional independence relation.

We will consider the following localized versions of extension and uniqueness:

Definition 9.7
.

Let
\mathbf{m}
be an independent
I
-system and let
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
be a skeleton of
\mathfrak{K}
.

(1)

We call
\mathbf{m}
a
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
-extension base in
\mathfrak{i}
if
J\in\mathcal{I},J=I\cup\{v\}
and
I<v
, there exists an independent
J
-system
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
such that
v
is
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
-proper in
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{m}\cong\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I
. When
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}=\mathfrak{K}
and
\mathfrak{i}
is clear from context, we omit them and call
\mathbf{m}
an
extension base
.

(2)

We call
\mathbf{m}
a
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
-strong uniqueness base in
\mathfrak{i}
if
J\in\mathcal{I},J=I\cup\{v\}
and
I<v
and any two independent
J
-systems
\mathbf{m}_{1},\mathbf{m}_{2}
such that
\mathbf{m}_{1}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I=\mathbf{m}_{2}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I=\mathbf{m}
and
v
is
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
-proper in both
\mathbf{m}_{1}
and
\mathbf{m}_{2}
, we have that
\mathbf{m}_{1}\cong_{\mathbf{m}}\mathbf{m}_{2}
. As before, when
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}=\mathfrak{K}
and
\mathfrak{i}
is clear from context, we omit may them.

(3)

We call
\mathbf{m}
a
uniqueness base in
\mathfrak{i}
if for any
J\in\mathcal{I}
such that
J=I\cup\{v\}
and
I<v
and any two independent
J
-systems
\mathbf{m}_{1},\mathbf{m}_{2}
such that
\mathbf{m}_{1}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I=\mathbf{m}_{2}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I=\mathbf{m}
, we have that there is an
\mathfrak{i}
-independent
J
-system
\mathbf{m}^{\ast}
and
f_{\ell}:\mathbf{m}_{\ell}\xrightarrow[\mathbf{m}]{}\mathbf{m}^{\ast}
systems embeddings for
\ell=1,2
. When
\mathfrak{i}
is clear from context, we may omit it.

It turns out that the parameter
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
in the definition of a
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
-extension base can safely be omitted. This will be used without comments:

Lemma 9.8
.

Let
\mathbf{m}
be an
\mathfrak{i}
-independent system and let
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
be a skeleton of
\mathfrak{K}
. Let
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}\coloneqq\mathfrak{i}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
.

(1)

\mathbf{m}
is a
\mathfrak{K}
-extension base in
\mathfrak{i}
if and only if
\mathbf{m}
is a
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
-extension base in
\mathfrak{i}
.

(2)

If
\mathbf{m}
is
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
-independent, then
\mathbf{m}
is a
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
-extension base in
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
if and only if
\mathbf{m}
is a
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
-extension base in
\mathfrak{i}
.

Proof.

Use monotonicity 2 and play with the definition of a skeleton (as in the proof of Lemma
8.5
).
∎

Of course, having extension is the same as all independent systems being extension bases, and similarly for the other properties:

Lemma 9.9
.

\mathfrak{i}
has extension [uniqueness] [strong uniqueness] if and only if every independent system is an extension [uniqueness] [strong uniqueness] base.

Proof.

Straightforward.
∎

Note also that the uniqueness properties transfers down from
\mathfrak{i}
to
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
:

Lemma 9.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
be a skeleton of
\mathfrak{i}
. If
\mathfrak{i}
has [strong] uniqueness, then
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
has [strong] uniqueness.

Proof.

Straightforward using the basic properties of a skeleton.
∎

The extension property also transfers. In fact, more can be said:

Lemma 9.11
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
be a skeleton of
\mathfrak{i}
. Write
\mathfrak{K}\coloneqq\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}
,
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}\coloneqq\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}}
. Assume that
\mathfrak{i}
has extension. Let
I,J\in\mathcal{I}
with
I
an initial segment of
J
. Let
\mathbf{m}
be an
\mathfrak{i}
-independent
I
-system. There exists an
\mathfrak{i}
-independent
J
-system
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
such that:

(1)

\mathbf{m}\cong\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,J
.

(2)

\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
is
(J\backslash I)
-
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
-proper (see Definition
8.2
).

In particular,
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
has extension.

Proof.

Play with skeletons as in the proof of Lemma
8.5
.
∎

We now turn to the harder problem of getting the properties in
\mathfrak{i}
if we know them in the skeleton. For extension, this can be done provided that the underlying class is the same (the ordering may be different). The example to keep in mind is
\mathfrak{K}
being the class of saturated models of cardinality
\lambda
in a given AEC, and
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
consisting of the same class, but ordered by “being limit over”. Note however that the theorem below also has content when
\mathfrak{i}=\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
: it says that it is enough to prove that
proper
systems are extension bases.

Theorem 9.12
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
be a skeleton of
\mathfrak{i}
such that
|\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}}|=|\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}|
. If every proper
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
-independent system is an extension base, then
\mathfrak{i}
has extension.

Proof.

Write
\mathfrak{K}\coloneqq\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}
,
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}\coloneqq\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}}
. For
\mathbf{m}
an
\mathfrak{i}
-independent
I
-system, let
k(\mathbf{m})
be the number of
u\in I
which are
not

\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
-proper in
\mathbf{m}
. We prove that every
\mathfrak{i}
-independent system
\mathbf{m}
is an extension base by induction on
k(\mathbf{m})
.

Let
\mathbf{m}
be an independent
I
-system and let
J=I\cup\{v\}\in\mathcal{I}
, with
I<v
. If
k(\mathbf{m})=0
, then
\mathbf{m}
is an extension base by assumption. Assume now that
k(\mathbf{m})>0
. Let
u\in I
be such that
u
is
not

\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
-proper in
\mathbf{m}
. Note that
u\neq\bot
(minimal elements are proper). Let
I_{0}\coloneqq[u,\infty)_{I}
and let
\mathbf{m}_{0}\coloneqq\mathbf{m}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I_{0}
. Note that:

(A)

I_{0}\subseteq I
and
I_{0}\times\{0,1\}\in\mathcal{I}
(by (
1
) in the definition of a multidimensional independence relation), hence also
I_{0}\in\mathcal{I}
(see
8.9
, it is isomorphic to an initial segment of
I_{0}\times\{0,1\}
).

(B)

I\backslash I_{0}
is an initial segment of
I
, hence is in
\mathcal{I}
.

(C)

k(\mathbf{m}_{0})<k(\mathbf{m})
, since
u
is minimal in
I_{0}
, hence, since
|\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}|=|\mathfrak{K}|
,
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
-proper in
\mathbf{m}_{0}
(we are also using that properness is preserved when passing to subsystems, i.e. Lemma
8.4
).

Let
\mathbf{m}_{0}^{\prime}
be an
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
-independent proper
I_{0}
-system such that
\mathbf{m}_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{i}}\mathbf{m}_{0}^{\prime}
.

[Why? Let
\langle v_{\ell}:\ell<|I|\rangle
lists
I_{0}
with no repetition such that
v_{\ell}<_{I}v_{k}\Rightarrow\ell<k
and for
k\leq|I|,
let
I_{k}\coloneqq I\,{\upharpoonright}\,\{v_{\ell}:\ell<k\}.
Now by induction on
k\leq|I|
we choose
\mathbf{b}_{k}^{\prime}
such that:

(a)

\mathfrak{b}_{k}^{\prime}
is a
(J_{k}\times\{0,1\},\iota)
-system,

(b)

\mathfrak{b}_{k}^{\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,J_{k}\times\{u\}
is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{b}_{k}\,{\upharpoonright}\,J_{k}
over
\pi:J_{k}\times\{0\}\cong J_{k},

(c)

\mathbf{m}_{k}^{\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,(J_{k}\times\{1\})
is
\iota^{\ast}
-independent from
I_{k}\times\{1\}
-system.

We can carry the induction the induction hypothesis and for
k=|I|
we fulfill the promise above.]

Write
\mathbf{m}_{0}^{\prime}=\langle N_{u}:u\in I_{2}\rangle
. For
u\in I
, let
M_{u}^{\prime}\coloneqq N_{u}
if
u\notin I_{0}
and
M_{u}^{\prime}\coloneqq N_{u}
otherwise. Let
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\coloneqq\langle M_{u}^{\prime}:u\in I\rangle
. By monotonicity 5,
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
is
\mathfrak{i}
-independent. Moreover,
k(\mathbf{m}^{\prime})<k(\mathbf{m})
because
u
is proper in
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
. By the induction hypothesis,
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
must be an extension base. Let
\mathbf{m}^{\prime\prime}
be an independent
J
-system such that
v
is proper in
\mathbf{m}^{\prime\prime}
and there is
f:\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\cong\mathbf{m}^{\prime\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I
. Let
\mathbf{m}^{\prime\prime\prime}
be the unique
J
-system so that
\mathbf{m}^{\prime\prime\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\{v\}=\mathbf{m}^{\prime\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\{v\}
and
f\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathbf{m}\cong\mathbf{m}^{\prime\prime\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I
. By monotonicity 5,
\mathbf{m}^{\prime\prime\prime}
is independent, and hence witnesses that
\mathbf{m}
is an extension base.
∎

This implies a way to obtain extension from existence, provided that the skeleton satisfies strong uniqueness (in case
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}}
consists of limit models, ordered by being limit over, it turns out that in reasonable cases strong uniqueness will hold in
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
, though there is no hope of it holding in
\mathfrak{i}
).

Corollary 9.13
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
be a skeleton of
\mathfrak{i}
with
|\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}}|=|\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}|
. If
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
has strong uniqueness and existence, then
\mathfrak{i}
has extension.

Proof.

By Theorem
9.12
, it suffices to show that every proper
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
-independent
I
-system
\mathbf{m}
is an extension base. So let
J=I\cup\{v\}\in\mathcal{I}
with
I<v
. By existence, there is a proper
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
-independent
J
-system
\mathbf{m}^{\ast}
. By strong uniqueness,
m^{\ast}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I
is isomorphic to
\mathbf{m}
, and clearly
\mathbf{m}^{\ast}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I
is an extension base, so
\mathbf{m}
must be one as well.
∎

We now turn to studying under what conditions uniqueness in a skeleton implies uniqueness in the original independence relation. We are unable to prove the full analog of Theorem
9.12
, so we make the additional assumption that
\mathcal{I}
is closed under certain products. The following is the key lemma:

Lemma 9.14
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
be a skeleton of
\mathfrak{i}
. Assume that
\mathfrak{i}
has extension. Let
J\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}}
be such that
J=I\cup\{v\}
, for
I<v
. If
J\times\{0,1\}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}}
and any proper
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
-independent
I
-system is a uniqueness base, then any
\mathfrak{i}
-independent
I
-system is a uniqueness base.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{m}=\langle M_{u}:u\in I\rangle
be an
\mathfrak{i}
-independent
I
-system. Let
\mathbf{m}_{1},\mathbf{m}_{2}
both be
\mathfrak{i}
-independent
J
-systems,
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}=\langle M_{u}^{\ell}:u\in J\rangle
,
\ell=1,2
and without loss of generality
\mathbf{m}_{1}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I=\mathbf{m}_{2}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I=\mathbf{m}
. We want to find an amalgam of
M_{v}^{1}
and
M_{v}^{2}
fixing
\mathbf{m}
.

Let
J^{\prime}\coloneqq J\times\{0,1\}
,
I^{\prime}\coloneqq I\times\{0,1\}
. Identify
J
with
J\times\{0\}
. By Lemma
9.11
, we can find an
I^{\prime}
-independent system
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I=\mathbf{m}
which is
(I^{\prime}\backslash I)
-
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
-proper. For
\ell=1,2
, let
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}^{\prime}
be the
I^{\prime}\cup\{(v,0)\}
-system obtained by adding
M_{v}^{\ell}
to
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
. By monotonicity 2 and 3,
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}^{\prime}
is still independent. By extension and some renaming, one can find a
J^{\prime}
-independent system
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,(I^{\prime}\cup\{(v,0)\})=\mathbf{m}_{\ell}^{\prime}
. Moreover, we can arrange that
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}
is
(J^{\prime}\backslash J)
-
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
-proper. Now let
J^{\ast}\coloneqq J\times\{1\}
,
I^{\ast}\coloneqq I\times\{1\}
. We have that
\mathbf{m}^{\ast}\coloneqq\mathbf{m}_{1}^{\prime\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I^{\ast}=\mathbf{m}_{2}^{\prime\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I^{\ast}
. Moreover for
\ell=1,2
,
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}^{\ast}\coloneqq\mathbf{m}_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,J^{\ast}
is a proper
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
-independent system by construction. Thus it is a uniqueness base, so
\mathbf{m}_{1}^{\ast}
and
\mathbf{m}_{2}^{\ast}
amalgamate over
\mathbf{m}^{\ast}
. This implies that
M_{v}^{1}
and
M_{v}^{2}
amalgamate over
\mathbf{m}
, as desired.
∎

Theorem 9.15
.

Assume that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}}
is closed under products. Let
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
be a skeleton of
\mathfrak{i}
with
|\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}|=|\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}}|
. If
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
has strong uniqueness and existence, then
\mathfrak{i}
has uniqueness and extension.

Proof.

By Corollary
9.13
,
\mathfrak{i}
has extension. We have to see that for every
I\in\mathcal{I}
, every
\mathfrak{i}
-independent
I
-system is a uniqueness base but this is immediate from Lemma
9.14
.
∎

The following more local result will be used in the proof of Theorem
10.16
. It says roughly that if a uniqueness base is changed by making one of the models on the boundary smaller, then it is still a uniqueness base. A version of it appears in
[
She09a
, III.12.26]
.

Lemma 9.16
.

Let
\mathbf{m}=\langle M_{u}:u\in I\rangle
be an independent
I
-system. Let
u^{\ast}\in I
be maximal. Let
N_{u^{\ast}}\in\mathfrak{K}
be such that
M_{u^{\ast}}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N_{u^{\ast}}
. Let
\mathbf{m}^{\ast}\coloneqq\mathbf{m}\,{\upharpoonright}\,(I\backslash\{u^{\ast}\})\smallfrown N_{u^{\ast}}
. If
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits(\mathfrak{i})
has extension and
\mathbf{m}^{\ast}
is a uniqueness base, then
\mathbf{m}
is a uniqueness base.

Proof.

Let
J=I\cup\{v\}\in\mathcal{I}
,
I<v
. Let
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}=\langle M_{u}^{\ell}:u\in J\rangle
for
\ell=1,2
be independent systems with
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I=\mathbf{m}
for
\ell=1,2
. We want to amalgamate
M_{v}^{1}
and
M_{v}^{2}
over
\mathbf{m}
. Let
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits\coloneqq\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits(\mathfrak{i})
.

Using the extension property for
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
, find
f_{\ell},N_{v}^{\ell}
,
\ell=1,2
such that
f_{\ell}:N_{u^{\ast}}\xrightarrow[M_{u^{\ast}}]{}N_{v}^{\ell}
and
M_{v}^{\ell}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{u^{\ast}}}^{N_{v}^{\ell}}f_{\ell}[N_{u^{\ast}}]
. Let
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}^{\prime}\coloneqq\langle(M_{u}^{\ell})^{\prime}:u\in J\rangle
be defined by
(M_{u}^{\ell})^{\prime}\coloneqq M_{u}
if
u\in I\backslash\{u^{\ast}\}
,
(M_{u^{\ast}}^{\ell})^{\prime}\coloneqq f_{\ell}[N_{u^{\ast}}]
, and
(M_{v}^{\ell})^{\prime}\coloneqq N_{v}^{\ell}
. By monotonicity 4,
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}^{\prime}
is an independent system.

Let
f\coloneqq f_{2}f_{1}^{-1}\,{\upharpoonright}\,(M_{u^{\ast}}^{1})^{\prime}
. Then
f:\mathbf{m}_{1}^{\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I\cong\mathbf{m}_{2}^{\prime}I
and since
\mathbf{m}_{1}^{\prime}\cong\mathbf{m}^{\ast}
and
\mathbf{m}^{\ast}
is a uniqueness base, we know that there exists
g
extending
f
amalgamating
N_{v}^{1}
and
N_{v}^{2}
. Such an
f
also amalgamates
M_{v}^{1}
and
M_{v}^{2}
over
\mathbf{m}
, as desired.
∎

10.
The multidimensional amalgamation properties

Throughout this section, we continue to assume:

Hypothesis 10.1
.

\mathfrak{i}=(\mathfrak{K},\mathcal{I},\operatorname{NF})
is a multidimensional independence relation.

We study the existence and uniqueness properties restricted to systems indexed by
\mathcal{P}(n)
, for some
n<\omega
. We show (Lemma
10.9
, Theorem
10.10
) that once we have those, we can (under reasonable conditions), obtain the properties for systems indexed by other sets. We also give a relationship between extension and existence using strong uniqueness, akin to Corollary
9.13
but stronger: we only use strong uniqueness for smaller systems. This is Theorem
10.11
. Finally, we give sufficient conditions for the
n
-dimensional properties in terms of amalgamation properties inside classes of
n
-dimensional systems. The most important of these result is Theorem
10.16
.

It will be convenient to restrict oneself to systems indexed by finite initial segments of
\mathcal{P}(\omega)
. Independence relations considering only such systems will be called
okay
. We then define the
n
-dimensional properties by saying that they must hold for systems indexed by initial segments of
\mathcal{P}(n)
.

Definition 10.2
.

(1)

For
n<\omega
, let
\mathcal{I}_{n}
be the class of all partial orders isomorphic to an initial segment of
\mathcal{P}(n)
. Let
\mathcal{I}_{<\omega}\coloneqq\bigcup_{n<\omega}\mathcal{I}_{n}
.

(2)

We say that
\mathfrak{i}
is
okay
if
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{<\omega}
. We say that
\mathfrak{i}
is
n
-okay
if it is okay and
\mathcal{P}(n)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}}
.

(3)

For
P
a property from Definition
8.16
and
n<\omega
, we say that
\mathfrak{i}
has
n
-
P
if
\mathfrak{i}
is
n
-okay and
\mathfrak{i}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{I}_{n}
has
P
. We say that
\mathfrak{i}
has
(<n)
-
P
if it has
m
-
P
for all
m<n
.

(4)

For
P
a property,
\lambda
an infinite cardinal and
n<\omega
, we say that
\mathfrak{i}
has
(\lambda,n)
-
P
if
\mathfrak{i}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}
has
n
-
P
. We say that
\mathfrak{i}
has
(\lambda,<n)
-
P
if it has
(\lambda,m)
-
P
for all
m<n
. Similarly define variations like the
(\Theta,n)
-
P
, for
\Theta
an interval of cardinals.

Remark 10.3
.

Since
\mathfrak{i}
is closed under initial segments, if
\mathfrak{i}
is
n
-okay then
\mathcal{I}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{I}
, so
\mathfrak{i}
is
m
-okay for any
m<n
.

Note that
\mathfrak{i}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{I}_{<\omega}
is always an okay independence relation, so replacing
\mathfrak{i}
by
\mathfrak{i}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{I}_{<\omega}
, we assume for the rest of this section that
\mathfrak{i}
is okay:

Hypothesis 10.4
.

\mathfrak{i}
is
okay
.

It is easy to characterize the low-dimensional amalgamation properties in terms of familiar properties:

Lemma 10.5
.

(1)

\mathfrak{i}
has
0
-existence if and only if
\mathfrak{i}
has
0
-extension if and only if
\mathfrak{K}\neq\emptyset
.

(2)

\mathfrak{i}
has strong
0
-uniqueness if and only if
\mathfrak{K}
has at most one model up to isomorphism.

(3)

\mathfrak{i}
has
0
-uniqueness if and only if
\mathfrak{K}
has joint embedding.

(4)

\mathfrak{i}
has
1
-extension if and only if
\mathfrak{i}
is
1
-okay,
\mathfrak{K}
is not empty, and
\mathfrak{K}
has no maximal models.

(5)

\mathfrak{i}
has
1
-uniqueness if and only if
\mathfrak{i}
is
1
-okay and
\mathfrak{K}
has amalgamation and joint embedding.

(6)

If
\mathfrak{i}
has
2
-extension, then
\mathfrak{K}
has disjoint amalgamation.

Proof.

Immediate.
∎

Interestingly, strong
1
-uniqueness together with uniqueness already implies strong uniqueness. In case we are considering classes of limit models ordered by being limit over, strong
1
-uniqueness is exactly the uniqueness of limit models, a key property studied in many papers
[
SV99
,
Van06
,
Van13
,
GVV16
,
Van16
]
.

Lemma 10.6
.

If
\mathfrak{i}
has strong
1
-uniqueness and uniqueness, then
\mathfrak{i}
has strong uniqueness.

Proof.

We use Lemma
9.9
. Let
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}=\langle M_{u}^{\ell}:u\in J\rangle
,
\ell=1,2
be independent
J
-systems. Let
v
be a top element of
J
and let
I\coloneqq J\backslash\{v\}
. Assume that
\mathbf{m}\coloneqq\mathbf{m}_{1}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I=\mathbf{m}_{2}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I
and
v
is proper in both
\mathbf{m}_{1}
and
\mathbf{m}_{2}
. We show that
M_{v}^{1}
and
M_{v}^{2}
are isomorphic over
\mathbf{m}
. By definition of being proper, we can pick
N_{v}^{\ell}<_{\mathfrak{K}}M_{v}^{\ell}
so that
M_{u}^{\ell}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}N_{v}^{\ell}
,
\ell=1,2
.
By uniqueness, there exists
M\in\mathfrak{K}
and
f_{\ell}:N_{v}^{\ell}\rightarrow M
with
f_{\ell}
fixing
\mathbf{m}
,
\ell=1,2
. If
|J|\leq 2
, there is nothing to prove so assume that
|J|>2
. Then
M_{v}^{1}
must be a proper extension of
M_{u}^{1}
for each
u<v
. By strong
0
-uniqueness,
\mathfrak{K}
must have no maximal models. Thus we can pick
N\in\mathfrak{K}
with
M<_{\mathfrak{K}}N
. By strong
1
-uniqueness and some renaming there exists
g_{\ell}:M_{v}^{\ell}\cong N
, with
g_{\ell}
extending
f_{\ell}
,
\ell=1,2
. In the end,
g\coloneqq g_{2}^{-1}g_{1}
is the desired isomorphism.
∎

We now want to show that it is enough to prove that
\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)
-systems are extension bases to get
n
-extension. To this end, the following invariance of a semilattice will be useful:

Definition 10.7
.

For
I\in\mathcal{I}_{<\omega}
and
u\in I
, let
n(I,u)
be the cardinality of
f(u)
, where
f:I\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\omega)
is any semilattice embedding such that
f[I]
is an initial segment of
\mathcal{P}(\omega)
. Let
n(I)
be the maximum of
n(I,u)
for all
u\in I
. When
I
is empty, we specify
n(\emptyset)=-1
.

Lemma 10.8
.

Let
n<\omega
and
I,J\in\mathcal{I}_{<\omega}
be non-empty.

(1)

If
I\subseteq J
, then
n(I)\leq n(J)
.

(2)

n(\mathcal{P}(n))=n
.

(3)

n(\mathcal{P}^{-}(n))=n-1
.

(4)

n(I\times\{0,1\})=n(I)+1
.

(5)

If
I\in\mathcal{I}_{n}
, then
n(I)\leq n
.

Proof.

Straightforward.
∎

Lemma 10.9
.

Let
I,J\in\mathcal{I}_{<\omega}
and let
I
be an initial segment of
J
. Let
n\coloneqq n(J)
.

(1)

If for every
m\leq n
, every proper
\mathcal{P}^{-}(m)
-system is an extension base, then every proper independent
I
-system can be extended to a proper independent
J
-system.

(2)

If for every
m\leq n
, every proper
\mathcal{P}^{-}(m)
-system is a strong uniqueness base, then for any proper independent
J
-systems
\mathbf{m}_{1},\mathbf{m}_{2}
and any isomorphism
f:\mathbf{m}_{1}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I\cong\mathbf{m}_{2}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I
, there is an isomorphism
g:\mathbf{m}_{1}\cong\mathbf{m}_{2}
extending
f
.

Proof.

Identify
J
with an initial segment of
\mathcal{P}(\omega)
.

(1)

Let
\mathbf{m}
be a proper independent
I
-system. Let
u\in J\backslash I
be such that
J_{0}\coloneqq I\cup\{u\}
is an initial segment of
J
. It is enough to show that
\mathbf{m}
can be extended to a proper independent
J_{0}
-system. Let
m\coloneqq|u|
. Note that
m\leq n
. Let
\pi:u\rightarrow m
be a bijection. Since
J_{0}
is an initial segment of
\mathcal{P}(\omega)
, We have that
\pi
induces an isomorphism from
(-\infty,u]=\mathcal{P}(u)
onto
\mathcal{P}(m)
. In particular,
\mathbf{m}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(u)
is an extension base. Now extend it to
\mathcal{P}(u)
and use monotonicity to argue that this induces an extension of
\mathbf{m}
to
J_{0}
.

(2)

Similar.

∎

Theorem 10.10
.

Let
n<\omega
and assume that
\mathfrak{i}
is
n
-okay. If for every
m\leq n
, every proper
\mathcal{P}^{-}(m)
-system is an extension base, then
\mathfrak{i}
has
n
-extension.

Proof.

Directly from Theorem
9.12
and Lemma
10.9
.
∎

The following result will be useful to obtain extension from existence and strong uniqueness for lower-dimensional systems:

Theorem 10.11
.

Let
n<\omega
and assume that
\mathfrak{i}
is
n
-okay. If
\mathfrak{i}
has strong
(<n)
-uniqueness, then
\mathfrak{i}
has
n
-existence if and only if
\mathfrak{i}
has
n
-extension.

Proof.

If
\mathfrak{i}
has
n
-extension, then by Lemma
9.3
it always has
n
-existence (this does not use strong uniqueness). Assume now that
\mathfrak{i}
has
n
-existence. By Theorem
10.10
, it suffices to show that for every
m\leq n
, every proper
\mathcal{P}^{-}(m)
-system is an extension base. Let
m\leq n
and let
\mathbf{m}
be a proper
\mathcal{P}^{-}(m)
-system. By
n
-existence, there is a proper
\mathcal{P}(m)
-system
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
. By strong
(<n)
-uniqueness and Lemma
10.9
,
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(m)\cong\mathbf{m}
. Since
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(m)
is an extension base, so is
\mathbf{m}
.
∎

We would like to establish a criteria to obtain uniqueness from a kind of amalgamation in the class
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathcal{P}(n)}
(Definition
8.15
). It turns out that a slightly finer version of
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathcal{P}(n)}
is useful for this purpose: the idea is that we require the interior of the system to be limit but allow the boundary to not extend the rest in limit way (of course this is formulated abstractly using skeletons).

Definition 10.12
.

(1) Let
n<\omega
and assume that
\mathfrak{i}
is
n
-okay. Let
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
be a skeleton of
\mathfrak{i}
. Let
K^{\ast}
be the class of proper
\mathfrak{i}
-independent
\mathcal{P}(n)
-systems
\mathbf{m}
such that
\mathbf{m}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)
is
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
-independent. Let
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}
be the abstract class whose underlying class is
K^{\ast}
and whose ordering is
\mathbf{m}_{1}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}}\mathbf{m}_{2}
if and only if
\mathbf{m}_{1}=\mathbf{m}_{2}
or whenever
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}=\mathbf{m}_{\ell}^{-}\smallfrown M_{\ell}
with
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}^{-}\coloneqq\mathbf{m}_{\ell}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)
,
\ell=1,2
, we have that
\mathbf{m}_{1}\leq_{\mathfrak{i}}\mathbf{m}_{2}
,
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are in
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}}
,
M_{1}<_{\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}}}M_{2}
, and
\mathbf{m}_{1}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)<_{\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)}}\mathbf{m}_{2}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)
.

(2) Assume
I\subsetneq J
are finite initial segments of
\mathcal{P}(\omega),n\coloneqq n(J)
and
I=\{u\in J:|u|<n\}.
We repeat the definition in part (1) with
I,J
here playing the roles of
\mathcal{P}^{-}(n),\mathcal{P}(n).

Remark 10.13
.

We have that
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}\subseteq\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}\subseteq\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathcal{P}(n)}
. In particular,
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i},\mathcal{P}(n)}=\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathcal{P}(n)}
.

Definition 10.14
.

For
n<\omega
, we let
\phi_{n}(x)
be the formula in the vocabulary of systems (Definition
8.8
) which holds inside a
\mathcal{P}(n)
-system
\langle M_{u}:u\in\mathcal{P}(n)\rangle
if and only if
a\in\bigcup_{u\subsetneq n}M_{u}
. That is,
\phi_{n}(x)
is
\bigvee_{u\subsetneq n}P_{u}(x),
where
P_{u}
is the predicate corresponding to
M_{u}.

The following easy relationships hold between the
n
-dimensional properties and classes of proper
n
-dimensional systems. See Definition
5.6
for the definitions of
\phi
-categoricity,
\phi
-amalgamation,
\phi
-uniqueness, etc.

Lemma 10.15
.

Let
n<\omega
. Assume that
\mathfrak{i}
is
(n+1)
-okay and let
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
be a skeleton of
\mathfrak{i}
.

(1)

For any
\mathbf{m}_{1}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}
, there exists
\mathbf{m}_{2}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}
such that
\mathbf{m}_{1}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}}\mathbf{m}_{2}
and
\mathbf{m}_{1}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)=\mathbf{m}_{2}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)
.

(2)

If
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
has strong
(<n)
-uniqueness, then
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}
is
\phi_{n}
-categorical.

(3)

If
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
has strong
n
-uniqueness, then any
\mathbf{m}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}
is
\phi_{n}
-universal in
(|\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}|,\leq_{\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}}^{d})
.

(4)

If
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
has strong
n
-uniqueness, then
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}
has
\phi_{n}
-uniqueness.

(5)

Let
\bar{\mathbf{m}}=(\mathbf{m}_{0},\mathbf{m}_{1},\mathbf{m}_{2})
be a
\phi_{n}
-span in
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}
, and write
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}=\langle M_{u}^{\ell}:u\in\mathcal{P}(n)\rangle
,
\ell=0,1,2
. Then
\bar{\mathbf{m}}
has a
\phi_{n}
-amalgam in
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}
if and only if there exists
N\in\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}
and
f_{\ell}:M_{\mathcal{P}(n)}^{\ell}\xrightarrow[\mathbf{m}_{0}]{}N
,
\ell=1,2
, such that
f_{1}\,{\upharpoonright}\,M_{u}^{1}=f_{2}\,{\upharpoonright}\,M_{u}^{2}
for all
u\in\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)
.

(6)

If
\mathbf{m}
is a
\phi_{n}
-amalgamation base in
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}
, then for any
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}
with
\mathbf{m}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}}\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
, we have that
(\mathbf{m}\ast\mathbf{m}^{\prime})\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(n+1)
is a uniqueness base in
\mathfrak{i}
.

Proof.

(1)

By the basic properties of a skeleton.

(2)

Since
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
has strong
(<n)
-uniqueness, any two
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
-independent
\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)
-systems are isomorphic.

(3)

By the first part and strong
n
-uniqueness.

(4)

Expanding the definitions, it suffices to show that if
\mathbf{m}_{1}
,
\mathbf{m}_{2}
are two
\mathcal{P}^{-}(n+1)
-systems such that
\mathbf{m}_{1}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)=\mathbf{m}_{2}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)
, then there is an isomorphism
f:\mathbf{m}_{1}\cong\mathbf{m}_{2}
fixing
\mathbf{m}_{1}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)
. This is implied by strong
n
-uniqueness, because
n(\mathcal{P}^{-}(n+1))=n
.

(5)

By monotonicity.

(6)

Expand the definitions.

∎

The following result (used in the proof of Lemma
11.16
) will be crucial: under the assumption of density of certain amalgamation bases in the class of
(n+1)
-dimensional systems, it implies that the
whole
independence relation has
(n+1)
-uniqueness. More precisely, we also assume that
n\geq 2
, work inside a skeleton, and assume that the skeleton has strong
n
-uniqueness and
(n+1)
-extension (in the case of interest, these will hold).

Theorem 10.16
.

Let
n\in[2,\omega)
. Let
\mathfrak{i}
be a multidimensional independence notion and let
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
be a skeleton of
\mathfrak{i}
. If:

(A)

\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
has
(n+1)
-extension and strong
n
-uniqueness.

(B)

For any
\mathbf{m}_{0}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}
, there exists a
\phi_{n}
-amalgamation base
\mathbf{m}_{1}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}
such that
\mathbf{m}_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}}\mathbf{m}_{1}
.

Then
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
has
(n+1)
-uniqueness.

Proof.

By Theorem
9.12
,
\mathfrak{i}
has
(n+1)
-extension. By Lemma
9.14
, the proof of Lemma
10.6
, and Lemma
10.9
, it suffices to show that every proper
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
-independent
\mathcal{P}^{-}(n+1)
-system is a uniqueness base(essentially we are reducing uniqueness to uniqueness for proper systems). Note that
n(\mathcal{P}^{-}(n+1))=n
, so by Lemma
10.9
again, any two proper
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
-independent
\mathcal{P}^{-}(n+1)
-systems are isomorphic. Thus it suffices to show that
some
proper
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
-independent
\mathcal{P}^{-}(n+1)
-system is a uniqueness base.

Pick any
\mathbf{m}_{0}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}
(exists by extension in
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
). By assumption, there is a
\phi_{n}
-amalgamation base
\mathbf{m}_{1}
in
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}
which extends
\mathbf{m}_{0}
. By
(n+1)
-extension, there exists
\mathbf{m}_{2}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}
such that
\mathbf{m}_{1}<_{\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}}\mathbf{m}_{2}
. By Lemma
10.15
(
6
),
\mathbf{m}\coloneqq(\mathbf{m}_{1}\ast\mathbf{m}_{2})\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(n+1)
is a uniqueness base (buy may not be proper, so we have to work more). Write
\mathbf{m}=\langle M_{u}:u\in\mathcal{P}^{-}(n+1)\rangle
and let
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}=\langle M_{u}^{\prime}:u\in\mathcal{P}^{-}(n+1)\rangle
be defined as follows: let
I_{0}\coloneqq\mathcal{P}^{-}(n+1)\backslash\{n\}
,
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I_{0}\coloneqq(\mathbf{m}_{0}\ast\mathbf{m}_{2})\,{\upharpoonright}\,I_{0}
,
M_{n}^{\prime}=M_{n}
. It is easy to check that
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
is an independent system and a uniqueness base as well: anytime a model
N
extends all the elements of
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
, it extends all the elements of
\mathbf{m}
.

Now let
\mathbf{m}^{\ast}
be a
\mathcal{P}^{-}(n+1)
-system defined as follows:
\mathbf{m}^{\ast}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I_{0}=\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I_{0}
and
\mathbf{m}^{\ast}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\{n\}=\mathbf{m}_{0}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\{n\}
. Note that then
\mathbf{m}^{\ast}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}(n)=\mathbf{m}_{0}
, and it is easy to check that
\mathbf{m}^{\ast}
is a proper
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
-independent system. Moreover,
\mathbf{m}^{\ast}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\{n\}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\{n\}
by definition of
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
, so by Lemma
9.16
,
\mathbf{m}^{\ast}
is a uniqueness base, as desired.
∎

11.
Continuous independence relations

This section is central. We define here the conditions a multidimensional independence relation should satisfy to be well-behaved with respect to increasing chains. In particular, it should be closed under union of those. We call such multidimensional independence relations
very good
. They are the ones we really want to study. We prove the
construction theorem
(Theorem
11.3
), which says that from a very good two-dimensional independence relation, we can build a very good multidimensional independence relation.

We study an important subclass of independent systems: the
limit
ones (Definition
11.7
). We show that they are quite well-behaved under unions and resolvability (Lemma
11.9
). There are some technical issues here, since we do not know that the class of
n
-dimensional limit systems is closed under unions until we have proven
(n+1)
-dimensional uniqueness, but we want to use the former in the proof of the latter. We use the class
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}
(Definition
10.12
) to get around this.

We prove the
n
-dimensional properties by studying the class of limit systems and using the weak diamond (or a strongly compact cardinal). Crucial is the
stepping up lemma
(Lemma
11.16
) which shows how to go from the
n
-dimensional properties to the
(n+1)
-dimensional properties (for limit systems). It leads directly to the
limit excellence theorem
(Theorem
11.17
) giving sufficient conditions for the
n
-dimensional properties. Of course, once we have the properties for limit systems we will be able to get them for regular systems using the results of Section
9
.

The first definition is the multidimensional analog of Definition
7.11
.

Definition 11.1
.

We say that a multidimensional independence relation
\mathfrak{i}
has
strong continuity
if whenever
\langle\mathbf{m}_{i}:i\leq\delta\rangle
is a
\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}
-increasing continuous chain of
(I,\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}})
-systems and
\mathbf{m}_{i}
is independent for all
i<\delta
, then
\mathbf{m}_{\delta}
is independent.

Definition 11.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}
be a multidimensional independence relation. We say that
\mathfrak{i}
is
very good
if:

(A)

\mathfrak{i}
is
n
-okay for all
n<\omega
(so
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}}=\mathcal{I}_{<\omega}
), see Definition
10.2
.

(B)

\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits(\mathfrak{i})
is very good.

(C)

\mathfrak{i}
has strong continuity.

(D)

For any
I\in\mathcal{I}
, both
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i},I}
and the class
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
of independent
I
-system ordered by
\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}^{d}
(see Definition
8.6
(
2
)) are fragmented AECs. Moreover the AECs they generate have the same models
12
12

12

This is a multidimensional analog of reflecting down (Definition
7.7
).
.

Very good multidimensional independence relations are obtained by taking a very good two-dimensional independence notion and applying the construction described in Definition
8.19
(and proven to have some good properties in Theorem
8.20
).

Theorem 11.3

(The construction theorem)
.

If
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
is a very good two-dimensional independence notion on
\mathfrak{K}
, then
13
13

13

Note that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}(\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits)}
is a class of latices, whereas
\mathcal{I}
is a countable set of latices included in it.

\mathfrak{i}(\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits)\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{I}_{<\omega}
(see Definition
10.2
) is a very good multidimensional independence notion.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{i}\coloneqq\mathfrak{i}(\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits)\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{I}_{<\omega}
. By Theorem
8.20
,
\mathfrak{i}
is a multidimensional independence notion. By definition,
\mathfrak{i}
is
n
-okay for all
n<\omega
and
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits({\mathfrak{i}})=\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
(see the statement of Theorem
8.20
), which is very good by assumption. It also follows from the definition of
\mathfrak{i}
that it has strong continuity, since
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
has it. It remains to see that for any
I\in\mathcal{I}
, both
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i},I}
and
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
(the class of independent
I
-systems ordered by
\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}^{d}
) are fragmented AECs that generate the same models. Now
\mathfrak{K}
is a fragmented AEC because
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
is very good. Thus by strong continuity it follows immediately that
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
is a fragmented AEC, and that
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i},I}
is a fragmented AEC similarly follows from the definition of
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
. It remains to check that those two classes generate the same class of models. Let
\lambda\coloneqq\min(\Theta)
, let
\mathfrak{K}^{1}
be the AEC generated by
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i},I}
, and let
\mathfrak{K}^{2}
be the AEC generated by
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
. Note that
|\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}^{1}|=|\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}^{2}|
, and
\leq_{\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}^{1}}=\leq_{(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i},I})_{\lambda}}
,
\leq_{\mathfrak{K}^{2}}=\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}^{d}
. Since
\leq_{\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}}
extends
\leq_{\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i},I}}
, we have that
|\mathfrak{K}^{1}|\subseteq|\mathfrak{K}^{2}|
. To see the other direction, let
\mathbf{m}\in\mathfrak{K}^{2}
. We claim that there exists
\mathbf{m}_{0}
in
\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}^{1}
such that for any subset
A
of
\mathbf{m}
of size
\lambda
there is
\mathbf{m}_{1}\in\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}^{1}
with
\mathbf{m}_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}^{1}}\mathbf{m}_{1}
,
\mathbf{m}_{1}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}^{2}}\mathbf{m}
, and
\mathbf{m}_{1}
containing
A
. This will suffice by the usual directed system argument. Suppose that the claim fails. We build
\langle\mathbf{m}_{i}:i<\lambda^{+}\rangle

\leq_{\mathfrak{K}^{2}}
-increasing continuous in
\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}^{1}
such that for all
i<\lambda
,
\mathbf{m}_{i}\not\leq_{\mathfrak{K}^{1}}\mathbf{m}_{i+1}
. This is possible by failure of the claim. This is enough: we know that
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
is very good, hence reflects down. From the definition of
\mathfrak{i}
, we get that there must be a club of
i<\lambda^{+}
such that
\mathbf{m}_{i}\leq_{\mathfrak{i},I}\mathbf{m}_{i+1}
, a contradiction.
∎

Note that a very good multidimensional independence relation has underlying class a fragmented AEC, which may possibly “shrink” (e.g. it could be a class of saturated models). The next lemma shows that the AEC generated by this fragmented AEC also carries a multidimensional independence relation (which is quite nice, but may fail to be very good because of the requirement that
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits(\mathfrak{i})
be very good, hence has extension and uniqueness):

Definition 11.4
.

We call a multidimensional independence relation
\mathfrak{i}

almost very good
if:

(A)

\mathfrak{i}_{\min(\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{i}))}
is very good.

(B)

\mathfrak{i}
satisfies all the properties in the definition of very good, except that
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits(\mathfrak{i})
may not be very good.

Lemma 11.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}
be a very good multidimensional independence notion. Let
\lambda\coloneqq\min(\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}))
. Then there exists a (unique) multidimensional independence notion
\mathfrak{i}^{\prime}
such that:

(A)

\mathfrak{i}_{\min(\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{i}))}
is very good.

(B)

\lambda=\min(\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\prime}}))
and
\mathfrak{i}_{\lambda}=\mathfrak{i}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
.

(C)

\mathfrak{i}^{\prime}
is almost very good.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{K}
be the AEC generated by
\left(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}\right)_{\lambda}
. More generally, for each
I\in\mathcal{I}
, let
\mathfrak{K}_{I}
be the AEC generated by
\left(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i},I}\right)_{\lambda}
. We let
\mathfrak{i}^{\prime}
be defined as follows:
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\prime}}=\mathfrak{K}
,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}}
, and for any
(\mathfrak{K},I)
-system
\mathbf{m}
,
\mathbf{m}
is
\mathfrak{i}^{\prime}
-independent if and only if
\mathbf{m}\in\mathfrak{K}_{I}
. It is a straightforward (but long) to check that
\mathfrak{i}^{\prime}
is indeed the desired multidimensional independence relation.
∎

Note that if we have all the
n
-dimensional properties at a fixed cardinal
\lambda
, we can transfer them up. More precisely:

Lemma 11.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}
be an almost very good multidimensional independence relation with domain
[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]
(for
\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}
) and let
n<\omega
.

(1)

If
\mathfrak{i}
has
([\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),n+1)
-extension, then
\mathfrak{i}
has
([\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}],n)
-extension.

(2)

If
\mathfrak{i}
has
([\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),n+1)
-uniqueness and
([\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),n+1)
-extension, then
\mathfrak{i}
has
([\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}],n)
-uniqueness.

In particular, if
\mathfrak{i}
has
(\lambda_{1},<\omega)
-extension and
(\lambda_{1},<\omega)
-uniqueness, then
\mathfrak{i}
has extension and uniqueness.

Proof.

This is quite standard, and the definitions are tailored to make this work, so we do not replay the arguments here. See
[
She83b
, §5]
for example. As a warmup, the reader may want to prove that no maximal model and disjoint amalgamation in
\lambda
imply no maximal models in
\lambda^{+}
(this is a combinatorial version of
(\lambda,2)
-extension implying
(\lambda^{+},1)
-extension - disjoint amalgamation plays the role of tow-dimensional independence).
∎

Limit systems are candidates for strong uniqueness, so are an important object of study. Really they are our main example of proper system and we are now finally shifting to specializing our results to them.

Definition 11.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}
be a very good multidimensional independence relation. We define a multidimensional independence relation
\mathfrak{i}^{\mathrm{lim}}
as follows:

(A)

\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\mathrm{lim}}}
consist of
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}^{\text{lim}}
, the class of limit models in
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}
, ordered by being limit over (see Definition
3.23
).

(B)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\mathrm{lim}}}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}}
.

(C)

\operatorname{NF}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\mathrm{lim}}}=\operatorname{NF}_{\mathfrak{i}}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\mathrm{lim}}}
.

We call a proper
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
-independent system a
limit

\mathfrak{i}
-independent system.

Definition 11.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}
be a very good multidimensional independence relation and let
\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
be a skeleton of
\mathfrak{i}
. For
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}}
, we let
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper},\ast}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},I}
be the sub-abstract class of
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},I}
(as in Definition
10.12
(2)) with the same ordering but consisting only of the systems where all models have the same cardinality. Similarly define
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper},\ast}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},I}
to be the sub-abstract class of
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},I}
(Definition
8.15
) consisting of systems with all models of the same cardinality.

The class of limit systems satisfies the following properties:

Lemma 11.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}
be a very good multidimensional independence relation with domain
\Theta
.

(1)

\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
is a skeleton of
\mathfrak{i}
.

(2)

For any
\lambda\in\Theta
,
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has strong
(\lambda,2)
-uniqueness and
(\lambda,2)
-existence.

(3)

For any subinterval
\Theta_{0}
of
\Theta
,
\left(\mathfrak{i}_{\Theta_{0}}\right)^{\mathrm{lim}}=\left(\mathfrak{i}^{\mathrm{lim}}\right)_{\Theta_{0}}
.

(4)

For any
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}}
,
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper},\ast}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}},I}
is a resolvable abstract class with LST number
\min(\Theta)
.

(5)

Let
\Theta^{-}\coloneqq\{\lambda\in\Theta\mid\lambda^{+}\in\Theta\}
.

(a)

Let
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}}
and let
n\coloneqq n(I)
. If for any
\lambda\in\Theta^{-}
, we have that
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has strong
(\lambda,n+1)
-uniqueness and
(\lambda,n+1)
-extension, then
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper},\ast}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},I}
is
\Theta^{-}
-continuous.

(b)

Let
n<\omega
. If for any
\lambda\in\Theta^{-}
, we have that
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has strong
(\lambda,n)
-uniqueness and
(\lambda,n)
-extension, then
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper},\ast}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},\mathcal{P}(n)}
is
\Theta^{-}
-continuous.

Proof.

(1) Directly from the definitions and the (easy to check) fact that
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}^{\mathrm{lim}}
is a skeleton of
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}
.

(2) Because
\mathfrak{i}
has it by definition of very good. That is, any relevant property for
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
is such property for
\mathfrak{i};
and applying the property for
\mathfrak{i}
we get there a solution and it is easy to “convert” it to a solution in
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}.

(3) Immediate from the definitions.

(4) We leave it to the reader to verify that the LST number is
\min(\Theta)
. We prove that
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper},\ast}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}},I}
is resolvable. We check that the conditions of Lemma
5.4
are satisfied. All the conditions are easy, except for resolvability for successors. So let
\mathbf{m}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper},\ast}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}},I}
have cardinality
\lambda^{+}
and let
\mathbf{m}_{0}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper},\ast}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}},I}}\mathbf{m}_{1}
have cardinality
\lambda
, with
\lambda,\lambda^{+}\in\Theta
. Since
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i},I}
is a fragmented AEC, it is resolvable, hence there exists
\langle\mathbf{m}_{i}:i<\lambda^{+}\rangle
a resolution of
\mathbf{m}
over
\mathbf{m}_{0}
in
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i},I}
. Write
\mathbf{m}_{i}=\langle M_{u}^{i}:u\in I\rangle
. What are we missing to make this a resolution in
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper},\ast}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}},I}
? We want the
\mathbf{m}_{i}
’s to be limit, and in fact we also want
\mathbf{m}_{i}\ast\mathbf{m}_{i+1}
to be limit. From the properties of being limit, it is in fact enough to show that there is a club
C
of
\lambda^{+}
such that
\langle\mathbf{m}_{i}:i\in C\rangle
is an increasing chain in
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper},\ast}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}},I}
. To get this club, first get a club
C_{1}
(using Lemma
5.3
) such that for
i<j
both in
C_{1}
,
M_{u}^{i}
is limit and
M_{u}^{j}
is limit over
M_{u}^{i}
(this is possible since
M_{u}^{\lambda^{+}}
is limit, hence saturated). Next, use Fact
7.10
on each pair of models in
\mathbf{m}
and intersect clubs to get that on a club
C
, for all
i\in C
,
M_{v}^{i}
is limit over
M_{u}^{i}
whenever
u<v
. This is not enough to make
\mathbf{m}_{i}
limit, as we need for each
u\in I
an intermediate
M
such that
M_{u}^{i}
is limit over
M
and
M
is limit over each
M_{v}^{i}
,
v<u
. Now by assumption
\mathbf{m}_{\lambda^{+}}
is limit, so for each
u\in I
there exists
M_{u}^{\ast}
with
M_{u}^{\ast}
limit over
M_{v}^{\lambda^{+}}
and
M_{u}^{\lambda^{+}}
limit over
M_{u}^{\ast}
. Resolve
\mathbf{m}_{\lambda^{+}}\smallfrown M_{u}^{\ast}
as before to obtain a club
C_{u}
and a resolution
\langle M_{u}^{\ast,i}:i\in C_{u}\rangle
of
M_{u}^{\ast}
such that
M_{u}^{\ast,i}
is limit over
M_{v}^{i}
for each
v<u
and
M_{u}^{i}
is limit over
M_{u}^{\ast,i}
. Note that we can further require that for
i<j
both in
C_{u}
,
M_{u}^{\ast,j}
is limit over
M_{u}^{\ast,i}
, hence the corresponding part of the system
\mathbf{m}_{i}\ast m_{j}
is also limit. Now intersect all the
C_{u}
’s and intersect further with
C_{1}
to get the desired club.

(5)
Clause (a)
: let
\langle\mathbf{m}_{i}:i<\delta\rangle
be an increasing chain in
\left(\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper},\ast}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},I}\right)_{\Theta^{-}}
. Let
\mathbf{m}_{\delta}\coloneqq\bigcup_{i<\delta}\mathbf{m}_{i}
. We have that
\mathbf{m}_{\delta}
is an independent system, the only issue is to prove that it is limit (that is, that the extensions between the models are limit). Once this is done, it will immediately follow that
\mathbf{m}_{\delta}
extends
\mathbf{m}_{0}
. Write
\mathbf{m}_{i}=\langle M_{u}^{i}:u\in I\rangle
. By Fact
7.9
, we directly have that
M_{v}^{\delta}
is limit over
M_{u}^{\delta}
for each
u<v
. However, we need more: for a fixed
u\in I
, we want
M_{u}^{\delta}
to be limit over
\bigcup_{v<u}M_{v}^{\delta}
. Recall (Definition
3.5
(
4
)) that this means that there is
M
such that
M
is limit over each
M_{v}^{\delta}
,
v<u
, and
M_{u}^{\delta}
is limit over
M
. In fact, it suffices to find
M^{\ast}
such that
M
extends each
M_{v}^{\delta}
,
v<u
and
M_{u}^{\delta}
is limit over
M^{\ast}
(then pick
M
limit over
M^{\ast}
such that
M_{u}^{\delta}
is limit over
M
).

We proceed by induction on
\delta
. So we can assume that
\delta=\text{cf}(\delta)
and the chain
\langle\mathbf{m}_{i}:i<\delta\rangle
is continuous. Fix
u\in I
and write
A_{i}\coloneqq\bigcup_{v<u}M_{v}^{i}
. We build
\langle M_{i}:i\leq\delta\rangle
an increasing continuous chain of limit models such that for all
i<\delta
:

(1)

M_{i+1}
is limit over
M_{i}
.

(2)

A_{i}\subseteq|M_{i}|
.

(3)

M_{u}^{i}
is limit over
M_{i}
.

(4)

M_{u}^{i}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{i}}^{M_{u}^{i+1}}M_{i+1}
(where
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits=\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits(\mathfrak{i})
).

This is enough: by Fact
7.9
,
M_{\delta}
is as desired. This is possible: the base case uses that
\mathbf{m}_{0}
is itself a limit system. At limits, take unions (and use Fact
7.9
). For the successor step, assume we are given
i<\delta
. Let
I_{0}\coloneqq\{v\in I\mid v\leq u\}
, and let
J\coloneqq I_{0}\cup\{v^{\ast}\}
, where
v^{\ast}
is a new element such that
v^{\ast}<u
and
v<v^{\ast}
for all
v\in I
with
v<u
.

Let
\mathbf{m}_{i}^{\ast}=\langle M_{v}^{\ast}:v\in J\rangle
be the
J
-system such that
\mathbf{m}_{i}^{\ast}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I_{0}=\mathbf{m}_{i}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I_{0}
and
M_{v^{\ast}}^{\ast}=M_{i}
. It is easy to check that this is a limit independent system. We now use
(n+1)
-extension to build a limit independent
J
-system
\mathbf{m}^{\ast\ast}
with
\mathbf{m}^{\ast}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}{\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper},\ast}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}},J}}\mathbf{m}^{\ast}
. We do this in two steps: first we build an
I
-system extending
\mathbf{m}^{\ast}\,{\upharpoonright}\,I
(using extension on
I\times\{0,1\}
, note that
n(I\times\{0,1\})=n(I)+1=n+1
), then using two-dimensional extension to complete this to an extension
\mathbf{m}^{\ast\ast}
. Now by strong
(n+1)
-uniqueness (again applied in two steps), we must have that there is
f:\mathbf{m}^{\ast\ast}\cong_{\mathbf{m}_{i}}\mathbf{m}_{i+1}
. Let
M_{i+1}\coloneqq f[M_{v^{\ast}}^{\ast\ast}]
, where
M_{v^{\ast}}^{\ast\ast}
is the
v^{\ast}
-indexed element of
\mathbf{m}^{\ast\ast}
.

Clause (b)
: immediate from the previous part applied to
I=\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)
.
∎

As hinted at before, limit models are well-behaved with respect to the
n
-dimensional properties: strong uniqueness is equivalent to uniqueness, and existence is equivalent to extension (assuming lower-dimensional uniqueness).

Lemma 11.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}
be a very good independence relation. Let
\lambda\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{i})
and let
n<\omega
. Then:

(1)
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has strong
(\lambda,n)
-uniqueness if and only if
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,n)
-uniqueness.

(2) If
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,<n)
-uniqueness, then
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,n)
-existence if and only if
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,n)
-extension.

Proof.

By Lemma
10.6
and Lemma
11.9
(
2
).

By Theorem
10.11
and the previous part.
∎

In compact classes, one can take an ultraproduct of limit systems and it will still be limit. In fact, we will see that the existence of such a uniform extension is very powerful, so we call independence relations with this property
nice
:

Definition 11.11
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}
be a very good multidimensional independence relation. For
\mathbf{m}_{1},\mathbf{m}_{2}\in\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i},I}
, say with
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}=\langle M_{u}^{\ell}:u\in I\rangle
we say that
\mathbf{m}_{2}
is
limit over
\mathbf{m}_{1}
if
\mathbf{m}_{1}\leq_{\mathfrak{i}}\mathbf{m}_{2}
and
M_{u}^{2}
is limit over
M_{u}^{1}
for all
u\in I
.

Definition 11.12
.

A very good multidimensional independence relation
\mathfrak{i}
is
nice
if:

(A)

\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}
is categorical in every
\lambda\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{i})
.

(B)

For any
n<\omega
and any two
\mathbf{m}_{1}
,
\mathbf{m}_{2}\in\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathcal{P}(n)}
with
\mathbf{m}_{1}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)=\mathbf{m}_{2}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)
, there exists independent
\mathbf{m}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{m}_{2}^{\prime}
such that:

(a)

\mathbf{m}_{\ell}^{\prime}
is limit over
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}
.

(b)

\mathbf{m}_{1}^{\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)=\mathbf{m}_{2}^{\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)
.

(c)

If
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}=\langle M_{u}^{\ell}:u\in\mathcal{P}(n)\rangle
,
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}^{\prime}=\langle N_{u}^{\ell}:u\in\mathcal{P}(n)\rangle
, then
\|M_{u}^{\ell}\|=\|N_{u}^{\ell}\|
for all
u\in\mathcal{P}(n)
and
\ell=1,2
.

Compact AECs have a nice independence notion:

Theorem 11.13
.

Let
\mathfrak{K}
be a compact AEC. If
\mathfrak{K}
is categorical in some
\mu>\text{LS}(\mathfrak{K})
, then there exists a
nice
very good multidimensional independence notion
\mathfrak{i}
with
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}=\mathfrak{K}^{\text{sat}}_{\geq\text{LS}(\mathfrak{K})^{+6}}
.

Proof.

By Fact
7.21
, there is a categorical very good frame
\mathfrak{s}
on
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{sat}}_{\geq\text{LS}(\mathfrak{K})^{+6}}
. Let
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
be the very good two-dimensional independence notion associated with
\mathfrak{s}
. Let
\mathfrak{i}\coloneqq\mathfrak{i}(\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits)
. By Theorem
11.3
,
\mathfrak{i}
is very good, we only have to check that it is nice. From the definition,
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}}
is indeed categorical in all cardinals, so we have to check the second clause in the definition of being nice. Let
\mathbf{m}_{1},\mathbf{m}_{2}
be as there. Write
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}=\langle M_{u}^{\ell}:u\in\mathcal{P}(n)\rangle
. Using
[
Bon14
, 4.11]
, we can build a
\kappa
-complete ultrafilter
U
such that for each
u\in\mathcal{P}(n)
and
\ell=1,2
, the
U
-ultrapower
N_{u}^{\ell}
of
M_{u}^{\ell}
(seen as an extension of
M_{u}^{\ell}
) is limit over
M_{u}^{\ell}
. Let
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}^{\prime}\coloneqq\langle N_{u}^{\ell}:u\in\mathcal{P}(n)\rangle
. We show that
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}\leq_{\mathfrak{i}}\mathbf{m}_{\ell}^{\prime}
. It is then not difficult to show, using a downward Löwenheim-Skolem-like argument, that one can take appropriate submodels of the
N_{u}^{\ell}
’s to be of the same size as the
M_{u}^{\ell}
’s and satisfy the requirements.

Essentially by
[
BG17
,
BGKV16
]
(and more precisely by the proof of
[
BGKV16
, 6.8]
), the two-dimensional independence relation on
\mathfrak{i}
is given by
\kappa
-coheir (see
[
BG17
, 3.2]
). Thus it follows from the claim before the proof of
[
BG17
, 8.2]
and some forking calculus that
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}\leq_{\mathfrak{i}}\mathbf{m}_{\ell}^{\prime}
.
∎

We now start working toward the key
stepping up lemma
, allowing us to move from a
(\lambda,n+1)
-property to a
(\lambda^{+},n)
-property. Existence is the easiest: to find a
(\lambda,n+1)
-system, one can simply resolve a
(\lambda^{+},n)
-system. This is formalized by the following lemma:

Lemma 11.14
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}
be a very good multidimensional independence notion with domain
\{\lambda,\lambda^{+}\}
. If
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda^{+},n)
-existence, then
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,n+1)
-existence.

Proof.

By
(\lambda^{+},n)
-existence, pick
\mathbf{m}
a limit independent
(\lambda^{+},\mathcal{P}(n))
-system. By Lemma
11.9
(
4
),
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper},\ast}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}},\mathcal{P}(n)}
is resolvable, so there exists a strictly increasing continuous chain
\langle\mathbf{m}_{i}:i<\lambda^{+}\rangle
in
\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}},\mathcal{P}(n)}
where for each
i<\lambda^{+}
,
\mathbf{m}_{i}
is a limit independent
(\lambda,\mathcal{P}(n))
-system, and
\mathbf{m}=\bigcup_{i<\lambda^{+}}\mathbf{m}_{i}
. Now by definition of
\leq_{\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper}}_{\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}},\mathcal{P}(n)}}
, we must have that
\mathbf{m}_{0}\ast\mathbf{m}_{1}
is a limit independent
(\lambda,\mathcal{P}(n+1))
-system, as desired.
∎

Thus we obtain the existence properties for free if the interval of cardinals we are working in is closed under the successor operation.

Lemma 11.15
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}
be a very good independence notion with domain
\Theta\coloneqq[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
. If
\lambda_{2}
is a limit cardinal, then for any
\lambda\in\Theta
,
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,<\omega)
-existence.

Proof.

We prove by induction on
n<\omega
that for any
\lambda\in\Theta
,
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,n)
-existence. When
n=0
, this is part of Lemma
11.9
. Assume now inductively that
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,n)
-existence for all
\lambda\in\Theta
. Let
\lambda\in\Theta
. Since
\lambda_{2}
is limit,
\lambda^{+}\in\Theta
. By the induction hypothesis,
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda^{+},n)
-existence. By Lemma
11.14
(applied to
\mathfrak{i}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\{\lambda,\lambda^{+}\}
),
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,n+1)
-existence, as desired.
∎

We now attack the harder question of obtain
(n+1)
-dimensional uniqueness from the
n
-dimensional properties. This is done using the weak diamond:

Lemma 11.16

(The stepping up lemma)
.

Let
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
be infinite cardinals such that
2^{\lambda_{1}}=2^{<\lambda_{2}}<2^{\lambda_{2}}
. Let
\mathfrak{i}
be a very good independence relation with domain
\Theta\coloneqq[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]
. Assume that for all
\lambda\in\Theta
,
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,n)
-existence and
(\lambda,n)
-uniqueness. Then:

(1) For all
\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
,
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,n+1)
-existence.

(2) There exists
\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
such that
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,n+1)
-uniqueness.

(3) If
\mathfrak{i}
is nice and
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda_{2},n+1)
-existence, then
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,n+1)
-uniqueness for
all

\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
.

Proof.

(1) By Lemma
11.14
applied to
\mathfrak{i}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\{\lambda,\lambda^{+}\}
.

(2) By Lemma
11.10
, for every
\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]
,
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has strong
(\lambda,n)
-uniqueness and
(\lambda,n)
-extension, and for every
\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
,
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
also has
(\lambda,n+1)
-extension. Write
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}\coloneqq\mathfrak{K}^{\text{proper},\ast}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}},\mathcal{P}(n)}
(Definition
11.8
).

Claim
: There is
N\in\mathfrak{K}_{[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}^{\ast}
which is a
\phi_{n}
-amalgamation base in
\mathfrak{K}_{\|N\|}^{\ast}
.

Proof of Claim
: Let
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast\ast}
be
|\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}|
ordered by
\mathbf{m}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}^{\ast\ast}}\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
if and only if
\mathbf{m}\leq_{\mathfrak{K}}^{d}\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
(Definition
8.6
(
2
)). We apply Theorem
5.8
, where
\mathfrak{K}
,
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast},\phi
there stand for
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
,
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast\ast},\phi_{n}
here (recalling Definition
10.14
). Note first that
\mathfrak{K}_{[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}^{\ast}
is not empty since
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,n+1)
-existence for each
\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
. Thus if we can check that the hypotheses of Theorem
5.8
, the
\iota=2
version, we will obtain the conclusion of the claim. First observe that
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
is
[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
-continuous by Lemma
11.9
(
5
). Next, there is a
\phi_{n}
-universal model in
\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda_{2}}^{\ast\ast}
by Lemma
10.15
(
3
). Further,
\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda_{2}}^{\ast\ast}
is
\phi_{n}
-categorical by Lemma
10.15
(
2
). Moreover, for any
\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
,
\mathfrak{K}_{\lambda}^{\ast}
has
\phi_{n}
-uniqueness (Lemma
10.15
(
4
)). Finally, for any
\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
, if we can
\phi_{n}
-amalgamate in
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast\ast}
, then we can
\phi_{n}
-amalgamate in
\mathfrak{K}^{\ast}
by Lemma
10.15
(
5
).
\dagger_{\text{Claim}}

Now let
\lambda\coloneqq\|N\|
, and apply Theorem
10.16
, where
\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast}
there stand for
\mathfrak{i}_{\lambda},(\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}})_{\lambda}
here. We get that
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,n+1)
-uniqueness, as desired.

(3) Assume that
\mathfrak{i}
is nice and
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda_{2},n+1)
-existence. It is enough to show that
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda_{1},n+1)
-uniqueness. Then given an arbitrary
\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
, we can run the same argument with
[\lambda,\lambda_{2}]
playing the role of
\Theta.
By the previous part, there is
\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
such that
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,n+1)
-uniqueness. Let
\mathbf{m}_{0}
be a limit independent
\mathcal{P}^{-}(n+1)
system in
\lambda_{1}
and let
\mathbf{m}_{1},\mathbf{m}_{2}
be limit independent
\mathcal{P}(n+1)
-systems in
\lambda_{1}
such that
\mathbf{m}_{1}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(n+1)=\mathbf{m}_{2}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(n+1)=\mathbf{m}_{0}
. We build
\langle\mathbf{m}_{\ell}^{i}:i\leq\lambda\rangle
strictly increasing continuous in
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i},\mathcal{P}(n+1)}
, such that for all
i<\lambda
:

(a)

\mathbf{m}_{\ell}^{0}=\mathbf{m}_{\ell}
,
\ell=1,2
.

(b)

All the models in
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}^{i}
have cardinality
\lambda_{1}+|i|
.

(c)

\mathbf{m}_{\ell}^{i+1}
is limit over
\mathbf{m}_{\ell}^{i}
, for
\ell=1,2
.

(d)

\mathbf{m}_{1}^{i}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(n+1)=\mathbf{m}_{2}^{i}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(n+1)
.

This is possible by definition of niceness (using that the extensions are limit, we can take unions at limits). Now let
\mathbf{m}^{\ast}\coloneqq\mathbf{m}_{1}^{\lambda}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(n+1)=\mathbf{m}_{2}^{\lambda}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(n+1)
. We claim that
\mathbf{m}^{\ast}
is a uniqueness base. From that it will directly follow that
\mathbf{m}_{0}
is also a uniqueness base, as desired. We would like to use that
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,n+1)
-uniqueness, but we can’t do so directly since
\mathbf{m}^{\ast}
may not be limit (all the models in the system are limit, but the extensions between the models may not be). We want to apply Lemma
9.14
, where
\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{\ast},J
there stand for
\mathfrak{i}_{\lambda}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{I}_{n+2}
,
(\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}})_{\lambda}\,{\upharpoonright}\,\mathcal{I}_{n+2},\mathcal{P}(n+1)
here. To apply it, we need to check that
\mathfrak{i}
has
(\lambda,n+2)
-extension.

By Lemma
9.12
and categoricity, it is enough to check that
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,n+2)
-extension. We know already that
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda^{\prime},n+1)
-existence for all
\lambda^{\prime}\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]
(when
\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda_{2}
, this is a hypothesis, and when
\lambda^{\prime}\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
, this was derived in the first part of this lemma). In particular,
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda^{+},n+1)
-existence. By Lemma
11.14
,
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,n+2)
-existence. Since
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,n+1)
-uniqueness, we get by Lemma
11.10
that
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,n+2)
-extension, as desired.
∎

We obtain two sufficient conditions to have all the
n
-dimensional properties: weak GCH or niceness.

Theorem 11.17

(The brimmed excellence theorem)
.

Let
\langle\lambda_{n}:n<\omega\rangle
be an increasing sequence of infinite cardinals such that
2^{\lambda_{n}}=2^{<\lambda_{n+1}}<2^{\lambda_{n+1}}
for all
n<\omega
. Let
\lambda_{\omega}\coloneqq\sup_{n<\omega}\lambda_{n}
and let
\Theta\coloneqq[\lambda_{0},\lambda_{\omega})
. Let
\mathfrak{i}
be a very good independence notion with domain
\Theta
. Assume that at least one of the following conditions holds:

(1)

\mathfrak{i}
is nice.

(2)

\lambda_{n+1}=\lambda_{n}^{+}
for all
n<\omega
.

Then for all
\lambda\in\Theta
,
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,<\omega)
-existence and
(\lambda,<\omega)
-uniqueness.

Proof.

By Lemma
11.15
(where
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
there stand for
\lambda_{0},\lambda_{\omega}
here), for any
\lambda\in\Theta
,
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,<\omega)
-existence. Next, we prove by induction on
n<\omega
that for all
\lambda\in\Theta
,
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,n)
-uniqueness. When
n=0
, this is part of Lemma
11.9
. Assume now that for all
\lambda\in\Theta
,
\mathfrak{i}
has
(\lambda,n)
-uniqueness. Fix
k<\omega
. By Lemma
11.16
(where
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
there stand for
\lambda_{k},\lambda_{k+1}
here),
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,n+1)
-uniqueness for all
\lambda\in[\lambda_{k},\lambda_{k+1})
. Since
k
was arbitrary, this shows that
\mathfrak{i}^{\text{brim}}
has
(\lambda,n+1)
-uniqueness for all
\lambda\in\Theta
.
∎

12.
Building primes

In this section, we show that prime models over independent systems can be built, provided that we have the
n
-dimensional extension and uniqueness properties. This is the
prime extension theorem
, Theorem
12.5
. Recall that a model is prime over a base if it is “minimal” over that base in some sense (see Definition
13.8
). It is well known that prime models are important to categoricity proof. The intuition is that they allow us to describe a model as being built “point by point”, analogous to how a vector space is spanned by a basis.

To build primes, we need the following technical concept, which says that a system can in a sense only be extended in an independent way. This is similar to the definition of a
reduced tower

[
SV99
, 3.1.11]
. The differences are that we work with systems and are slightly more local (we work inside a given system).

Definition 12.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}=(\mathfrak{K},\mathcal{I},\operatorname{NF})
be a multidimensional independence relation and let
\mathbf{m}
be an independent
I
-system, with
I=J\cup\{v\}
,
J<v
. Let
\mathbf{m}_{\ast}
be an independent
J
-system. We say that
\mathbf{m}
is
reduced inside
\mathbf{m}_{\ast}
if:

(1)

\mathbf{m}\,{\upharpoonright}\,J\leq_{\mathfrak{i}}\mathbf{m}_{\ast}
.

(2)

Whenever
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
is an independent
I
-system and
f\colon\mathbf{m}\rightarrow\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
is a systems embedding, if:

(a)

f
is the identity on
J
.

(b)

\mathbf{m}\,{\upharpoonright}\,J\leq_{\mathfrak{i}}\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,J
.

(c)

\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\,{\upharpoonright}\,J\leq_{\mathfrak{i}}\mathbf{m}_{\ast}
.

Then
f(\mathbf{m})\leq_{\mathfrak{i}}\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
.

Reduced systems exist:

Lemma 12.2

(Existence of reduced systems)
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}
be a very good multidimensional independence notion. Let
\Theta\coloneqq\operatorname{dom}(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}})
. Let
I=J\cup\{v\}
be in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{i}}
,
J<v
. Assume that
I
is an initial segment of
\mathcal{P}(n)
. Let
\lambda\in\Theta
be such that
\lambda^{+}\in\Theta
and
\mathfrak{i}
has
(\lambda,n+1)
-extension.

For any independent
J
-system
\mathbf{m}
in
\mathfrak{i}_{\lambda^{+}}
, there exists a
\leq_{\mathfrak{i}}
-increasing chain of
I
-systems
\langle\mathbf{m}_{i}:i<\lambda^{+}\rangle
in
\mathfrak{i}_{\lambda}
such that each
\mathbf{m}_{i}
is reduced in
\mathbf{m}
and
\bigcup_{i<\lambda^{+}}\mathbf{m}_{i}\,{\upharpoonright}\,J=\mathbf{m}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality,
\lambda=\min(\Theta)
. Let
\mathfrak{i}^{\prime}
be as given by Lemma
11.5
. Let
\langle a_{i}:i<\lambda^{+}\rangle
be an enumeration of
\mathbf{m}
. We build
\langle\mathbf{m}_{i}^{0}\colon i<\lambda^{+}\rangle
a
\leq_{\mathfrak{i}}
-increasing continuous chain of independent
J
-systems in
\mathfrak{i}_{\lambda}
,
\langle N_{i}\colon i<\lambda^{+}\rangle
in
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{i}_{\lambda}}
, and
